		<title>Adding Network Security To AI, HPC, And Data Centers</title>
		<link>https://semiengineering.com/adding-network-security-to-ai-hpc-and-data-centers-2/</link>
					<comments>https://semiengineering.com/adding-network-security-to-ai-hpc-and-data-centers-2/#respond</comments>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Maxim Demchenko]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Thu, 05 Nov 2026 08:06:45 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Blogs - Security]]></category>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>The location of security functions such as MACsec or IPsec has become a key architectural decision.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/adding-network-security-to-ai-hpc-and-data-centers-2/">Adding Network Security To AI, HPC, And Data Centers</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>AI and HPC are transforming data center networking. Modern infrastructures typically consist of a front-end network connecting users, storage, and management systems, and a back-end network interconnecting GPUs and compute nodes. At the largest scale, AI clusters span multiple locations, relying on scale-across networking to create a unified infrastructure.</p>
<p>While most modern data centers use leaf-spine architectures, implementation choices are influenced by more than technical requirements. Existing vendor relationships, product availability, supply-chain considerations, and total cost of ownership all play important roles. Consequently, organizations pursuing similar objectives often implement the network based on their business priorities, operational constraints, and upgrade strategies.</p>
<p>AI and HPC infrastructures process valuable data and represent significant financial investments. Protecting these environments requires controls for:</p>
<ul>
<li>Access control and authentication</li>
<li>Data confidentiality through encryption</li>
<li>Isolation between users, tenants, and workloads</li>
<li>Detection of tampering, replay, and other threats</li>
</ul>
<p>At the same time, security mechanisms must support the fundamental goals of AI infrastructure:</p>
<ul>
<li>Full line-rate throughput and minimal latency impact</li>
<li>Scalability across large numbers of ports and nodes</li>
<li>Acceptable power, silicon area, and deployment cost</li>
</ul>
<p>The industry benefits from more than a decade of operational experience with technologies such as MACsec and IPsec in high-speed networks. The primary challenge today is determining where and how those capabilities should be implemented.</p>
<p>Modern AI networks operate at hundreds of gigabits per second and are rapidly moving toward multi-terabit speeds. At these performance levels, network security must be implemented in following way:</p>
<ul>
<li>The security <strong>data plane</strong> performs real-time packet processing and protection. These functions are integrated into switching ASICs, SmartNICs, DPUs, optical DSPs, or interface devices.</li>
<li>The <strong>control plane</strong> implements security policy management, session establishment and run-time operations like periodic encryption key refresh.</li>
</ul>
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" fetchpriority="high" decoding="async" class="alignnone size-full wp-image-24283745" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Rambus_Adding-Network-Security-AI-HPC-fig1.png?resize=1378%2C520&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="1378" height="520" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Rambus_Adding-Network-Security-AI-HPC-fig1.png?w=1378&amp;ssl=1 1378w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Rambus_Adding-Network-Security-AI-HPC-fig1.png?resize=300%2C113&amp;ssl=1 300w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Rambus_Adding-Network-Security-AI-HPC-fig1.png?resize=1024%2C386&amp;ssl=1 1024w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Rambus_Adding-Network-Security-AI-HPC-fig1.png?resize=768%2C290&amp;ssl=1 768w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Rambus_Adding-Network-Security-AI-HPC-fig1.png?resize=600%2C226&amp;ssl=1 600w" sizes="(max-width: 1000px) 100vw, 1000px" /></p>
<p><strong>Fig. 1: Hardware-accelerated network security architecture with data plane and control plane.</strong></p>
<p>One of the most important architectural decisions is the location of security functions such as MACsec, IPsec, or emerging Ultra Ethernet security mechanisms.</p>
<p>Integrating security directly into the <strong>switch ASIC</strong> allows encryption and authentication to become part of the packet-forwarding pipeline.</p>
<p>Advantages:</p>
<ul>
<li>Native integration with packet classification and forwarding.</li>
<li>Broad flexibility in optical and pluggable module selection.</li>
</ul>
<p>Challenges:</p>
<ul>
<li>Consumes silicon area that could otherwise increase port density, packet buffering, switching capacity.</li>
<li>Security capabilities are tied to the ASIC lifecycle, limiting future upgrades.</li>
</ul>
<p>Alternatively, security functions can be implemented in <strong>external PHY devices </strong>(like the line-card retimer/gearboxes) or security-enabled pluggable modules.</p>
<p>Advantages:</p>
<ul>
<li>Easier introduction of new security capabilities.</li>
<li>Preserves switch ASIC resources for networking functions.</li>
<li>Moves extra power consumption away from the main ASIC and use the power optimized technology.</li>
</ul>
<p>Challenges:</p>
<ul>
<li>Creates dependencies on external vendors and module availability.</li>
<li>Shifts the security boundary from the chip level to the front-panel level.</li>
</ul>
<p>AI and compute systems rely on specialized transport protocols, including InfiniBand, RoCEv2, Ultra Ethernet Transport (UET). Unlike fabric-level security, transport security is typically associated with a specific user, job, or workload and therefore operates as an end-to-end service. Security must remain transparent to the underlying fabric while preserving the low latency and high throughput required by accelerated computing.</p>
<p>For this reason, transport security is integrated into (Smart) NICs and closely coupled with hardware-accelerated transport processing and often support multiple transport and security protocols simultaneously to accommodate diverse communication requirements.</p>
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" decoding="async" class="alignnone size-full wp-image-24283746" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Rambus_Adding-Network-Security-AI-HPC-fig2.png?resize=1389%2C442&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="1389" height="442" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Rambus_Adding-Network-Security-AI-HPC-fig2.png?w=1389&amp;ssl=1 1389w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Rambus_Adding-Network-Security-AI-HPC-fig2.png?resize=300%2C95&amp;ssl=1 300w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Rambus_Adding-Network-Security-AI-HPC-fig2.png?resize=1024%2C326&amp;ssl=1 1024w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Rambus_Adding-Network-Security-AI-HPC-fig2.png?resize=768%2C244&amp;ssl=1 768w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Rambus_Adding-Network-Security-AI-HPC-fig2.png?resize=600%2C191&amp;ssl=1 600w" sizes="(max-width: 1000px) 100vw, 1000px" /></p>
<p><strong>Fig. 2: Network security in the AI NIC.</strong></p>
<p>Modern infrastructures deploy multiple security layers simultaneously based on topology, trust boundaries, and workload requirements:</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>MACsec</strong> for Layer 2 protection</li>
<li><strong>IPsec</strong> for Layer 3 security</li>
<li><strong>UET-TSS</strong> for Ultra Ethernet transport security</li>
<li><strong>Proprietary security protocols</strong> optimized for specific fabrics and deployments</li>
</ul>
<p>These mechanisms are largely complementary rather than competitive. MACsec is commonly used for link-level protection, IPsec secures routed traffic across networks, and transport-level security protects AI and HPC communication flows.</p>
<p>Advances in optics are expanding deployment options for network security. MACsec is now being integrated directly into 800G and 1.6T optical modules, enabled by use of silicon technologies optimized for low-power packet processing.</p>
<p>Looking ahead, chiplet-based switch and NIC architectures, together with near-package and co-packaged optics, may create new opportunities for security integration. Dedicated I/O chiplets could become a natural location for encryption and authentication functions while preserving valuable compute and switching resources.</p>
<p>Network security is now a fundamental requirement for AI, HPC, and cloud-scale data centers. Protecting traffic at terabit speeds demands hardware-based implementation capable of maintaining line-rate performance while meeting cost and power targets.</p>
<p>The industry offers multiple deployment options, including switch ASICs, external PHYs, optical modules, SmartNICs, and future chiplet-based architectures. This flexibility allows data center designers to balance security, performance, power efficiency, and system cost according to the needs of their infrastructure.</p>
<p>Rambus addresses these challenges through a broad portfolio of industry-proven network security silicon IP supporting rates up to 1.6T, and 3.2T in the future. Customers can balance security features, throughput, power consumption, and integration effort to deploy network security capabilities that meet the performance, cost, and time-to-market requirements of their silicon devices. You can read more about this topic in the white paper in the Links section below.</p>
<h3>Links</h3>
<ul>
<li><a href="https://go.rambus.com/adding-network-security-to-ai-hpc-and-data-centers">White Paper &#8211; Adding Network Security to AI, HPC and Data Centers</a></li>
<li><a href="https://www.rambus.com/security/protocol-engines/">Rambus Secure Networking IP</a></li>
</ul>

		<item>
		<title>Turning Conv2D Into A Practical GPU Compute Pattern</title>
		<link>https://semiengineering.com/turning-conv2d-into-a-practical-gpu-compute-pattern/</link>
					<comments>https://semiengineering.com/turning-conv2d-into-a-practical-gpu-compute-pattern/#respond</comments>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Mario Florea]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Thu, 05 Nov 2026 08:05:35 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Blogs - Security]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[IoT and Security]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Sharpening Your Edge]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[AI]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[CNNs]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[convolution]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[convolutional neural networks]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[GPUs]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Imagination Technologies]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[machine learning]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[ML]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">https://semiengineering.com/?p=24283724</guid>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>A reusable optimization mindset for engineers building GPU compute kernels.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/turning-conv2d-into-a-practical-gpu-compute-pattern/">Turning Conv2D Into A Practical GPU Compute Pattern</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>Convolution is one of the foundational operations in modern machine learning, but it is also a useful example of a broader engineering idea: many apparently specialized tensor operations can be reorganized into well-understood compute patterns that are easier to implement, optimize, and scale.</p>
<p>This article explains a practical way to implement 2D convolution by treating it as a structured form of matrix multiplication. The goal is not to promote a specific product or benchmark result, but to show a reusable optimization mindset for engineers building GPU compute kernels.</p>
<p><em><strong>Key idea</strong>: A 1×1 convolution is effectively a matrix multiply, and larger K×K convolutions can be expressed as a sequence of matrix-like accumulations with careful attention to cache behavior and loop ordering.</em></p>
<h2>Why this matters</h2>
<p>Conv2D is everywhere in inference pipelines, image models, and feature extraction stages. Yet the implementation challenge is often less about the mathematical definition and more about the execution strategy: how data is laid out, how work is grouped, and how memory traffic is controlled.</p>
<p>A useful approach is to avoid treating convolution as a completely unique primitive. Instead, interpret it as a compute pattern closely related to matrix multiplication. This can reduce implementation complexity and create a clearer path for optimization.</p>
<ul>
<li>It lowers engineering effort by reusing a familiar execution model.</li>
<li>It reduces maintenance cost because optimizations can transfer across related kernels.</li>
<li>It increases the chance that future hardware and software improvements benefit both matrix multiply and convolution paths.</li>
</ul>
<h2>Starting from the tensor shapes</h2>
<p>A standard Conv2D operation can be described as applying a learned filter tensor across an input tensor to produce an output tensor.</p>
<p><code>C = A * B</code></p>
<p><strong>Where:</strong><br />
<code>A</code> is the input tensor of size <code>N × H × W × C_in</code><br />
<code>B</code> is the weight tensor of size <code>H_filter × W_filter × C_in × C_out</code><br />
<code>C</code> is the output tensor of size <code>N × H_out × W_out × C_out</code></p>
<p>In practice, one of the most important simplifications comes from looking at the data in a way that matches the compute engine rather than the original mathematical notation.</p>
<h2>The simplest case: Why 1×1 convolution is special</h2>
<p>A 1×1 convolution is the cleanest place to see the matrix multiplication analogy. At each spatial position, the input contributes a feature vector of length <code>C_in</code>. The weights map that vector into an output vector of length <code>C_out</code>.</p>
<p>If the input tensor is already stored in NHWC order, each spatial feature vector is naturally arranged in a way that can be interpreted as rows of a matrix, without requiring explicit data rearrangement.</p>
<p><em><strong>Practical takeaway</strong>: For NHWC input, a 1×1 convolution can be treated directly as a matrix multiply from <code>C_in</code> to <code>C_out</code>, producing output in NHWC order with no transposition step.</em></p>
<p>This matters because unnecessary data movement is often one of the fastest ways to lose performance. When the original layout already matches the desired access pattern, the implementation can stay simple and efficient.</p>
<h2>From 1×1 to full K×K convolution</h2>
<p>Once the filter grows beyond 1×1, each output element depends on multiple spatial locations. A 3×3 convolution, for example, combines nine separate spatial contributions. Conceptually, this becomes a set of matrix-like products that are accumulated together.</p>
<p>That means the problem is no longer just “do matrix multiplication,” but “do repeated matrix-like accumulation while minimizing memory waste.”</p>
<p>For a 3×3 filter, there are nine <code>C_in</code> × <code>C_out</code> contributions per output location. A naive implementation can achieve good arithmetic utilization, but it may repeatedly flush and reload input data through the cache hierarchy. That is where execution order becomes critical.</p>
<h2>Loop ordering is an optimization decision</h2>
<p>One of the most useful lessons in convolution optimization is that equivalent mathematics can produce very different memory behavior.</p>
<p>A straightforward ordering may process each filter position as if it were a separate matrix multiply and accumulate the result. While this is simple, it can cause the same input region to be fetched repeatedly into L1 cache and potentially L2 as well.</p>
<p>Reordering loops can improve locality, reduce refetch traffic, and increase effective cache reuse. In other words, the arithmetic stays the same, but the memory system sees a much friendlier pattern.</p>
<p><em><strong>Optimization principle</strong>: When a kernel is already compute-valid, one of the highest-value changes is often to restructure loop nesting to improve data reuse before attempting low-level instruction tuning.</em></p>
<h2>A representative kernel pattern</h2>
<p>The following sample illustrates the core structure of the convolution loop nest. The details are implementation-specific, but the pattern is broadly recognizable: load weights, compute input coordinates, read vectorized input values, synchronize local memory, and accumulate partial products.</p>
<pre>1	VEC_TYPE out_vec = 0.0;  // accumulator for results, with _N_STRIDE length
2	while ((idx_x += 2) &lt; K_STEPS * 2) {
3	  for (int fh = 0; fh &lt; F_H; ++fh) {
4	    for (int fw = 0; fw &lt; F_W; ++fw) { 
5	     int idx_in2 = 
6	          IDX_FROM_4D(fh, fw, F_W, idx_x * 4 + load_in2_k_offset, CI, load_in2_n_offset, CO); 
7	     ((__local float *)localBuf)[local_idx] = (load_in2_n_offset &gt;= CO) ? 0 : in2[idx_in2];
8	 
9	      const int hi = h * STRIDE_H + fh * DILATION_H - PAD_TOP;
10	      const int wi = w * STRIDE_W + fw * DILATION_W - PAD_LEFT;
11	 
12	      idx_y = IDX_FROM_3D(b, hi, IN_H, wi, IN_W);
13	      input1_8.lo = (float4)read_imagef(in1, samp, (int2){idx_x, idx_y});
14	      input1_8.hi = (float4)read_imagef(in1, samp, (int2){idx_x + 1, idx_y});
15	      barrier(CLK_LOCAL_MEM_FENCE);
16	      batch8_F32_MM(input1_8, (__local float16 *)localBuf, (float2 *)&amp;out_vec,
17	                    (float2 *)&amp;out_vec);
18	      barrier(CLK_LOCAL_MEM_FENCE);
19	    }
20	  }
21	}
</pre>
<p>This kind of kernel is useful to study because it highlights where performance usually lives:</p>
<ul>
<li>Vectorized reads instead of scalar traffic</li>
<li>Local-memory staging for reusable weights</li>
<li>Structured accumulation over filter positions</li>
<li>Explicit synchronization around shared data</li>
<li>Thread-level parallelism across multiple outputs</li>
</ul>
<h2>Padding, stride, and boundary handling</h2>
<p>Real convolution kernels are shaped not only by filter math, but also by parameters such as padding, stride, and dilation. These affect both correctness and performance.</p>
<h3>Padding strategy</h3>
<p>Zero padding is the most common approach and is the one reflected in the source implementation. A typical boundary-safe pattern checks whether the computed input coordinate falls within valid image bounds before issuing the read.</p>
<pre>1	float8 input1_8 = 0;
2	if ((hi &gt;= 0) &amp;&amp; (hi &lt; IN_H) &amp;&amp; (wi &gt;= 0) &amp;&amp; (wi &lt; IN_W)) 
3	{
4	  input1_8[i] = { ... }
5	}
</pre>
<p>This is correct and easy to reason about, but a branch inside a hot loop can be expensive. One alternative is to move boundary handling outside the innermost loop by redirecting out-of-range reads to a dummy zero-filled line or buffer. That trades branch cost for setup logic and is often worth testing.</p>
<p><em><strong>Important</strong>: Boundary checks are not just a correctness concern. Their placement in the loop nest can have a measurable effect on throughput.</em></p>
<h3>Stride and filter size</h3>
<p>Filter size and stride also shape performance in less obvious ways.</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Larger filters </strong>generally increase input reuse, which can improve cache efficiency.</li>
<li><strong>Larger strides </strong>reduce reuse across neighboring outputs, which can make memory behavior less favorable.</li>
</ul>
<p>These effects are often indirect rather than absolute. A larger filter does not automatically make a kernel faster, and a larger stride does not automatically make it slow. But both influence the balance between arithmetic work and memory traffic, which is exactly where many Conv2D kernels are won or lost.</p>
<h2>What engineers can learn from this approach</h2>
<p>The most transferable value in this Conv2D implementation is not a single micro-optimization. It is the method behind it.</p>
<ul>
<li>Start from a clear tensor interpretation.</li>
<li>Identify when the operation maps naturally onto matrix multiplication.</li>
<li>Preserve data layout whenever possible to avoid unnecessary movement.</li>
<li>Focus early optimization effort on loop ordering and cache reuse.</li>
<li>Treat padding, stride, and boundary logic as performance-relevant design choices.</li>
</ul>
<p>This approach tends to produce implementations that are easier to reason about and easier to evolve. It also creates a stronger foundation for future acceleration, because many hardware platforms already invest heavily in dense linear algebra patterns.</p>
<h2>Common pitfalls</h2>
<p>When turning Conv2D into a matrix-like GPU kernel, several mistakes come up repeatedly:</p>
<ul>
<li>Assuming arithmetic cost is the main bottleneck when memory traffic is actually dominant</li>
<li>Introducing explicit data transforms too early, before checking whether the source layout is already suitable</li>
<li>Keeping boundary branches inside the hottest loop without measuring their impact</li>
<li>Optimizing instruction details before validating cache behavior and access ordering</li>
<li>Ignoring how stride changes data reuse patterns</li>
</ul>
<p><em><strong>Caution</strong>: A mathematically elegant implementation is not always a performant one. Equivalent formulations can behave very differently on real hardware.</em></p>
<h2>A practical mental model</h2>
<p>If you are designing or reviewing a GPU convolution kernel, it helps to think in three layers:</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Math layer</strong>: What values need to be multiplied and accumulated?</li>
<li><strong>Layout layer</strong>: How are those values arranged in memory, and can that layout be reused directly?</li>
<li><strong>Execution layer</strong>: In what order should work run so caches, local memory, and vector units are used efficiently?</li>
</ul>
<p>Strong kernels usually come from aligning all three layers, not from over-optimizing only one of them.</p>
<h2>Final takeaway</h2>
<p>Conv2D is often presented as a special-purpose deep learning primitive, but from an implementation perspective it can often be understood as structured matrix multiplication plus careful accumulation across spatial neighborhoods.</p>
<p>That framing is powerful because it simplifies design decisions. It encourages reuse of proven compute patterns, reduces unnecessary data motion, and puts optimization effort where it usually matters most: locality, loop order, and bandwidth efficiency.</p>
<p>For engineers working on GPU inference and tensor kernels, that is the bigger lesson. Good performance does not begin with heroic low-level tuning. It begins with choosing the right interpretation of the problem.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/turning-conv2d-into-a-practical-gpu-compute-pattern/">Turning Conv2D Into A Practical GPU Compute Pattern</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></content:encoded>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>Accurate SI analysis must account for interactions across vertical interconnects, interposers, silicon bridges, package transitions, and neighboring high-speed channels. </p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/a-system-level-approach-to-protecting-3d-ic-signal-integrity/">A System-Level Approach To Protecting 3D-IC Signal Integrity</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p><strong>By Muhammad Hassan, Mahmoud Mahany, and Mohamed Saleh</strong></p>
<p>The semiconductor industry is rapidly transitioning to chiplet-based architectures to meet the performance, bandwidth, and power demands of AI and high-performance computing (HPC). Technologies such as TSMC CoWoS, SoIC, Samsung I-Cube, X-Cube, Intel EMIB, and Foveros enable designers to integrate multiple compute, memory, and I/O dies into a single package, dramatically increasing system capability while reducing development risk.</p>
<p>As these architectures become more complex, signal integrity has emerged as a critical design challenge. High-speed die-to-die (D2D) interfaces, dense microbump arrays, silicon interposers, TSVs, and advanced package substrates introduce electromagnetic effects that can significantly impact performance, power efficiency, and product yield.</p>
<p>Traditional design methodologies that verify dies, packages, and interconnects independently are no longer sufficient. Success requires a system-level approach capable of validating complete communication channels spanning dies, interposers, package substrates, and memory stacks.</p>
<h2>Why signal integrity is challenging in 3D-IC/advanced packaging</h2>
<p>Signal integrity ensures reliable communication between heterogeneous chiplets and HBM stacks operating at high data rates. Unlike traditional 2D SoCs, 2.5D/3D systems—such as TSMC Chip-on-Wafer-on-Substrate with a Silicon Interposer (CoWoS), SoIC, and Chip-on-Wafer (CoW), together with Intel Embedded Multi-Die Interconnect Bridge (EMIB), EMIB-T, and Foveros technologies—feature multiple interconnect structures, including  D2D interfaces, microbumps, TSVs, RDLs, interposers, bridges, and advanced substrates. Each transition introduces impedance discontinuities, parasitic coupling, and return-path disturbances that cross process nodes and dense interconnects. Mitigating these effects requires a system-level view of the entire communication channel rather than isolated analysis of individual blocks.</p>
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" decoding="async" class="alignnone size-full wp-image-24283708" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Siemens_Protecting-3D-IC-SI-fig1-systems.png?resize=1570%2C296&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="1570" height="296" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Siemens_Protecting-3D-IC-SI-fig1-systems.png?w=1570&amp;ssl=1 1570w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Siemens_Protecting-3D-IC-SI-fig1-systems.png?resize=300%2C57&amp;ssl=1 300w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Siemens_Protecting-3D-IC-SI-fig1-systems.png?resize=1024%2C193&amp;ssl=1 1024w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Siemens_Protecting-3D-IC-SI-fig1-systems.png?resize=768%2C145&amp;ssl=1 768w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Siemens_Protecting-3D-IC-SI-fig1-systems.png?resize=1536%2C290&amp;ssl=1 1536w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Siemens_Protecting-3D-IC-SI-fig1-systems.png?resize=600%2C113&amp;ssl=1 600w" sizes="(max-width: 1000px) 100vw, 1000px" /></p>
<p><strong>Fig. 1: TSMC’s CoWoS-S on the left and Intel’s EMIB on the right.</strong></p>
<h2>Advanced packaging increases electromagnetic complexity</h2>
<p>Advanced packaging platforms introduce sophisticated, highly coupled routing topologies. At multi-gigahertz frequencies, accurate SI analysis must account for interactions across vertical interconnects, interposers, silicon bridges, package transitions, and neighboring high-speed channels. Unlike traditional PCBs, D2D interfaces exhibit distinct mathematical loss properties.</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Linear frequency roll-off</strong>: Loss scales steeply and linearly as skin-effect dominates the microscopic wires.</li>
<li><strong>Phase distortion (dispersion)</strong>: Channels shift from low-frequency resistive (RC) behavior to high-frequency transmission line behavior, triggering severe inter-symbol interference (ISI).</li>
<li><strong>Crosstalk dominance</strong>: Sub-micron routing gaps 0.5 µm to 2 µm cause capacitive and inductive coupling to inject more noise than physical dielectric attenuation.</li>
<li><strong>S21 transition</strong>: Insertion loss plots show a downward roll-off, transitioning from low-frequency RC loss to high-frequency substrate-dominated attenuation.</li>
</ul>
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" loading="lazy" decoding="async" class="alignnone size-full wp-image-24283709" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Siemens_Protecting-3D-IC-SI-fig2-loss-profile.png?resize=1333%2C762&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="1333" height="762" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Siemens_Protecting-3D-IC-SI-fig2-loss-profile.png?w=1333&amp;ssl=1 1333w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Siemens_Protecting-3D-IC-SI-fig2-loss-profile.png?resize=300%2C171&amp;ssl=1 300w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Siemens_Protecting-3D-IC-SI-fig2-loss-profile.png?resize=1024%2C585&amp;ssl=1 1024w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Siemens_Protecting-3D-IC-SI-fig2-loss-profile.png?resize=768%2C439&amp;ssl=1 768w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Siemens_Protecting-3D-IC-SI-fig2-loss-profile.png?resize=600%2C343&amp;ssl=1 600w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 1000px) 100vw, 1000px" /></p>
<p><strong>Fig. 2: Typical lossy channel loss profile in silicon interposer.</strong></p>
<p>The channel&#8217;s low-pass frequency response heavily attenuates high-frequency harmonics, blunting the signal&#8217;s rise and fall times. In the time domain, this high-frequency roll-off manifests as severe ISI and increased jitter, directly shrinking the vertical eye height (Y) at the receiver pad as shown in Figure 3.</p>
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" loading="lazy" decoding="async" class="alignnone size-full wp-image-24283710" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Siemens_Protecting-3D-IC-SI-fig3-channel-bandwidth.png?resize=1262%2C748&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="1262" height="748" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Siemens_Protecting-3D-IC-SI-fig3-channel-bandwidth.png?w=1262&amp;ssl=1 1262w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Siemens_Protecting-3D-IC-SI-fig3-channel-bandwidth.png?resize=300%2C178&amp;ssl=1 300w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Siemens_Protecting-3D-IC-SI-fig3-channel-bandwidth.png?resize=1024%2C607&amp;ssl=1 1024w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Siemens_Protecting-3D-IC-SI-fig3-channel-bandwidth.png?resize=768%2C455&amp;ssl=1 768w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Siemens_Protecting-3D-IC-SI-fig3-channel-bandwidth.png?resize=600%2C356&amp;ssl=1 600w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 1000px) 100vw, 1000px" /></p>
<p><strong>Fig. 3: Received signal highlighting channel bandwidth limitation.</strong></p>
<h2>UCIe raises the bar</h2>
<p>The emergence of advanced D2D communication standards is placing even greater emphasis on early signal integrity verification. Universal Chiplet Interconnect Express (UCIe) is an open industry specification defining physical layers, protocols, compliance, and multi-vendor interoperability for 2D, 2.5D, and 3D packages. It offers up to sub-2 ns physical layer latency, a highly scalable modular architecture, and maximum cost-effectiveness by mixing heterogeneous chiplets. The specification fundamentally bifurcates into two packaging tiers.</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Advanced packaging</strong> (2.5D interposer/bridge): Optimizes for ultra-short reach (&lt;=2 mm) at up to 64 GT/s per pin (UCIe 3.0), utilizing 64 single-ended mainband data lanes per module. It routes signals unterminated at the receiver to hit an ultra-dense energy efficiency of 0.25 pJ/bit.</li>
<li><strong>Standard packaging</strong> (2D/laminate): Extends channel reach up to 25 mm at 16 to 32 GT/s per pin across 16 single-ended mainband data lanes per module. It utilizes 50-ohm parallel ground terminations at the receiver to dissipate long-trace reflections at an efficiency cost of 0.5 pJ/bit.</li>
</ul>
<p>Enforcing the UCIe open standard shifts validation onto strict hardware-level compliance protocols; channels must maintain an extraction frequency response (S₂₁) strictly above a mandatory voltage transfer function mask.</p>
<p>Figure 4 compares representative UCIe bump maps for the two packaging tiers: UCIe-AP (advanced packaging) and UCIe-SP (standard packaging), illustrating their different bump densities and Tx/Rx lane organizations.</p>
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" loading="lazy" decoding="async" class="alignnone size-full wp-image-24283711" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Siemens_Protecting-3D-IC-SI-fig4-UCIebump-maps.png?resize=985%2C910&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="985" height="910" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Siemens_Protecting-3D-IC-SI-fig4-UCIebump-maps.png?w=985&amp;ssl=1 985w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Siemens_Protecting-3D-IC-SI-fig4-UCIebump-maps.png?resize=300%2C277&amp;ssl=1 300w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Siemens_Protecting-3D-IC-SI-fig4-UCIebump-maps.png?resize=768%2C710&amp;ssl=1 768w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Siemens_Protecting-3D-IC-SI-fig4-UCIebump-maps.png?resize=600%2C554&amp;ssl=1 600w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 985px) 100vw, 985px" /></p>
<p><strong>Fig. 4: UCIe bump maps: (a) UCIe-AP bump map and (b) UCIe-SP bump map.</strong></p>
<h2>Complex interposer stackups and EDA translation challenges</h2>
<p>Unlike traditional fiberglass PCBs that tolerate minor thickness variations and use coarse trace geometries (&gt;50), silicon interposers utilize a non-laminate, planarized back-end stack-up derived from semiconductor foundry manufacturing. Microscopic copper traces (0.5 to 2 µm) are embedded within a perfectly flat matrix of deposited silicon dioxide (SiO2) or low-k dielectrics. Because the interposer bridges sub-micron chiplet nodes, any surface waviness or conformal warping causes lithographic mask misalignment and snaps the microscopic wires. Consequently, foundries rely on expensive chemical vapor deposition and chemical mechanical planarization to polish every single layer down to an absolute mirror-like plane.</p>
<p>Accurately modeling these structures requires automated workflows to ingest highly detailed stackup data. Foundries distribute this structural data in specialized semiconductor formats, like iRCX, MiPT, and ITF. Effortlessly converting these formats into native EDA electromagnetic (EM) solver inputs is highly challenging. The target EM software must map the 2D process profiles into precise 3D boundary-value solver definitions while maintaining computational efficiency. Attempting this translation manually introduces severe risks of human error, as omitted layer rules or misconfigured thickness parameters create critical modeling inaccuracies later in the signal integrity sign-off phase.</p>
<h2>GDSII/OASIS: Interposer layout and EM simulation challenges</h2>
<p>Silicon interposers utilize GDSII and OASIS formats because they are manufactured using standard semiconductor foundry lithography rather than mechanical PCB drilling and routing. These sub-micron features (0.5 to 2 µm trace widths) require the exact geometric language of the chip foundry optimized for advanced silicon manufacturing.</p>
<p>Importing raw foundry GDSII or OASIS layout data into 3D EM simulation engines triggers three severe performance bottlenecks:</p>
<ol>
<li><strong>Absence of 3D stack-up context</strong>: GDSII and OASIS are purely 2D polygon coordinate maps containing no data on Z-axis height, dielectric thickness, or material loss tangents. Engineers must manually merge a separate tech file (.tch, .ict, .xml) to construct the physical 3D model.</li>
</ol>
<ol>
<li><strong>Fractured polygon mesh explosion</strong>: Tape-out formats do not support true smooth curves. Circular micro-bumps and TSVs are fractured into hundreds of microscopic, sharp-edged trapezoids. This triggers a computational mesh explosion that overwhelms simulator memory (RAM) upon ingestion.</li>
<li><strong>Hierarchy versus simulation flattening</strong>: GDSII and OASIS compress file sizes using deeply nested hierarchies (referencing one macro cell thousands of times). Because EM field solvers must calculate the entire continuous electrical signal path and surrounding ground return paths, the tool must &#8220;flatten&#8221; these cells, completely stripping away the file&#8217;s native layout compression and causing massive data inflation.</li>
</ol>
<h2>Unified 3D-IC signal integrity solution</h2>
<p>Siemens’ Innovator3D IC solution suite addresses these complexities by providing a unified, system-level environment that aggregates several proven technologies into a seamless workflow spanning architecture exploration, electromagnetic extraction, channel simulation, protocol compliance verification, and final system-level signoff.</p>
<ul>
<li>Innovator3D IC Integrator for rapid multi-die architectural floorplanning and integration</li>
<li>Innovator3D IC Integrator Layout for physical layout design, substrate trace pathing, and assembly closure</li>
<li>Innovator3D IC Protocol Analyzer for automated D2D interface compliance checking</li>
<li>HyperLynx SI/PI for comprehensive high-speed channel and power integrity simulation</li>
<li>xCalibrate for automated foundry stackup extraction and tech-file generation</li>
<li>Calibre DESIGNrev for high-performance layout manipulation and property annotation</li>
<li>Aprisa for tapeout-quality digital physical implementation and routing</li>
</ul>
<p><strong> <img data-recalc-dims="1" loading="lazy" decoding="async" class="alignnone size-full wp-image-24283712" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Siemens_Protecting-3D-IC-SI-fig5-Signal-Integrity-workflows.png?resize=1290%2C456&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="1290" height="456" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Siemens_Protecting-3D-IC-SI-fig5-Signal-Integrity-workflows.png?w=1290&amp;ssl=1 1290w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Siemens_Protecting-3D-IC-SI-fig5-Signal-Integrity-workflows.png?resize=300%2C106&amp;ssl=1 300w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Siemens_Protecting-3D-IC-SI-fig5-Signal-Integrity-workflows.png?resize=1024%2C362&amp;ssl=1 1024w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Siemens_Protecting-3D-IC-SI-fig5-Signal-Integrity-workflows.png?resize=768%2C271&amp;ssl=1 768w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Siemens_Protecting-3D-IC-SI-fig5-Signal-Integrity-workflows.png?resize=600%2C212&amp;ssl=1 600w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 1000px) 100vw, 1000px" /></strong></p>
<p><strong>Fig. 5: Signal integrity workflows for 3D IC using Innovator3D IC Integrator cockpit.</strong></p>
<p>As AI and HPC architectures rapidly adopt chiplet designs built on advanced platforms—such as TSMC CoWoS, SoIC, and Intel EMIB, EMIB-T, and Foveros—signal integrity verification must evolve from component-level validation to full system-level channel analysis. High-speed D2D interfaces like open-standard UCIe and proprietary NVLink-C2C demand high-precision EM modeling, strict protocol compliance verification, and package-aware channel optimization.</p>
<p>Siemens delivers a comprehensive, unified signal integrity platform. This end-to-end workflow enables early architectural exploration, accurate 3D EM extraction, automated stackup compilation, and robust protocol compliance verification—ensuring confident, first-time-right signoff for next-generation 2.5D and 3D IC designs.</p>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>Compression can shrink AI models, but efficient edge deployment starts with matching the architecture to the workload and hardware limits.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/fitting-ai-models-to-edge-applications/">Fitting AI Models To Edge Applications</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p><strong>Key Takeaways:</strong></p>
<ul>
<li>The first step in right-sizing an AI model for an embedded application is determining its purpose, including the target device functions and product features.</li>
<li>Designers typically use a mix of compression techniques rather than a single method. Strategies include quantization, distillation, and pruning.</li>
<li>Small language models (SLMs) and compact edge models require tight system-level integration to ensure optimal efficiency.</li>
</ul>
<hr />
<p>Today&#8217;s AI industry faces two very different challenges. On one side, enormous AI data centers and AI factories are seeking more efficient ways to process high-parameter large language models (LLMs), with bandwidth and memory as the key bottlenecks. On the other side are smaller AI models running locally on edge devices, where there isn&#8217;t enough area, power, or memory for complex, higher-parameter LLMs.</p>
<p>&#8220;This has led to a gold rush with people raising huge amounts of capital to build mega data centers, but has also sparked innovation on the edge,&#8221; said Ed Plowman, CTO at <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/imagination-technologies/">Imagination Technologies</a>. &#8220;People are trying to work out how they can cram an entire container of liquid into a very small cup, and that has been driven by the fact that compute resources are not the main problem at the moment. It&#8217;s bandwidth, and that&#8217;s why RAM has gotten so expensive. That triggered a whole bunch of researchers to look at what the key problems are and what we can do about it. That triggered innovations around quantization — reducing activations and weights simultaneously — to make the models even smaller but still maintaining the output capability of the model.&#8221;</p>
<p>AI models come in many shapes and sizes to fit a wide range of edge devices, from earbuds to phones, home appliances, industrial IoT, robotics, and automotive systems. Designers need to choose a model with the right number of parameters because an oversized model can slow the device down or cause it to overheat. On the flip side, if the model is too small, the product may not be smart enough.</p>
<p>The first step in right-sizing an AI model for an embedded application is to define its purpose. &#8220;You have to start from the point of view of what you want the model to do, but then there are a million and one things we can throw at it to make it smaller,&#8221; said Plowman. &#8220;Quantization and self-compression are very effective tools. Distillation is another approach, and it works better on transformers than on traditional convolutional neural networks (CNNs). All these things are starting to combine to get us to a point where the gold rush for bigger and bigger GPUs is less prevalent. The enablement in embedded systems and the progression towards quite intelligent models being able to run on truly embedded platforms is becoming more realistic.&#8221;</p>
<div style="width: 100%; max-width: 525px; margin: 12px 0;">
<table style="width: 618px; table-layout: fixed; border-collapse: collapse; border: 2px solid #17365d; font-family: Arial, sans-serif; font-size: 12px; line-height: 1.25; margin: 0px; height: 234px;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 23%;" />
<col style="width: 38%;" />
<col style="width: 39%;" /> </colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th style="background: #4169E1; color: #fff; border: 1px solid #17365d; padding: 4px 5px; text-align: left; font-weight: bold;">Technique</th>
<th style="background: #4169E1; color: #fff; border: 1px solid #17365d; padding: 4px 5px; text-align: left; font-weight: bold;">How It Works</th>
<th style="background: #4169E1; color: #fff; border: 1px solid #17365d; padding: 4px 5px; text-align: left; font-weight: bold;">Key Characteristics</th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td style="padding: 4px 5px; font-weight: bold; border: 1px solid #17365d;">Distillation</td>
<td style="padding: 4px 5px; border: 1px solid #17365d;">Trains a smaller student model to mimic<br />
a larger teacher.</td>
<td style="padding: 4px 5px; border: 1px solid #17365d;">Transfers learned behavior into<br />
a smaller model.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="padding: 4px 5px; font-weight: bold; background: #f3f6fa; border: 1px solid #17365d;">Pruning</td>
<td style="padding: 4px 5px; background: #f3f6fa; border: 1px solid #17365d;">Removes low-impact weights, neurons,<br />
heads, or layers.</td>
<td style="padding: 4px 5px; background: #f3f6fa; border: 1px solid #17365d;">Structured (whole units) or unstructured<br />
(individual weights).</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="padding: 4px 5px; font-weight: bold; border: 1px solid #17365d;">Quantization</td>
<td style="padding: 4px 5px; border: 1px solid #17365d;">Uses fewer bits for weights<br />
and activations.</td>
<td style="padding: 4px 5px; border: 1px solid #17365d;">Retains network structure; correction<br />
limits accuracy loss.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="padding: 4px 5px; font-weight: bold; background: #f3f6fa; border: 1px solid #17365d;">Self-compression</td>
<td style="padding: 4px 5px; background: #f3f6fa; border: 1px solid #17365d;">Gradually reduces precision,<br />
potentially to zero bits.</td>
<td style="padding: 4px 5px; background: #f3f6fa; border: 1px solid #17365d;">Managed compression that can target<br />
proprietary hardware.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p><strong>Fig. 1: Common ways to compress AI model</strong></p>
</div>
<p>The model size must be considered alongside the system. &#8220;The hard part is designing a model that is sophisticated enough to solve the problem but small enough to fit the device,&#8221; said Magnus Gedda, CTO and chief product officer at Imagimob, an <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/infineon-technologies/">Infineon Technologies</a> company. &#8220;On top of that, the model usually shares memory and compute with the rest of the application, so the available resources are smaller than the spec sheet suggests. Optimization techniques like pruning and quantization can shrink a model, but they are applied after design and usually after training, and they cost some accuracy. You need to pick an initial architecture that is the right size from the start so that its optimized version still fits the device and meets the performance target. Getting those three things to line up — problem fit, size, accuracy after optimization — is where most of the effort goes.&#8221;</p>
<p>Others agree that system-level efficiency is the central challenge. &#8220;Edge AI is ushering in a shift from &#8216;largest model wins&#8217; to &#8216;experience per watt,'&#8221; said Steve Woo, distinguished inventor and fellow at <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/rambus-inc/">Rambus</a>. &#8220;Techniques such as compression, pruning, and lower-precision arithmetic help shrink models to fit local resources, but deployment remains difficult because memory capacity and bandwidth are often limiting factors. Getting a model onto the device is just the beginning. Making it run efficiently within tight power and latency budgets is where the engineering challenge begins.&#8221;</p>
<p>Choosing the wrong AI model can lead to extra work after deployment or a substandard product. &#8220;Once you have the optimized model, deployment is not that hard,&#8221; said Infineon&#8217;s Gedda. &#8220;The model is converted to C code, compiled for the target, and flashed to the device. The challenge is using the hardware to its full potential, so that inference is energy-efficient and low-latency. Edge hardware is extremely varied — MCUs, accelerators, NPUs, DSPs — and standards are somewhat lacking. So without the right tooling you end up with a generic, inefficient deployment, or a lot of manual work per target.&#8221;</p>
<p>These deployment constraints make model selection an application-specific exercise. The right architecture depends on the functions the device must perform and the workloads it must support.</p>
<p><strong>Functions and workloads determine the correct model</strong><br />
Edge device and physical AI workloads vary considerably across sectors, from medical to industrial, automotive, home, or personal. The specific modalities of the collected sensor data are the primary driver for selecting the optimal AI model type and size.</p>
<p>&#8220;Right-sized inference for physical AI is critical,&#8221; noted Mick Posner, senior product marketing group director for chiplets at <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/cadence-design-systems/">Cadence</a>, in a recent blog. &#8220;Intelligent physical systems do not have a one-size-fits-all compute profile. Some workloads require always-on, low-power inference. Others require higher-performance multimodal processing across cameras, radar, lidar, audio, environmental sensors, and system-state data.&#8221;</p>
<p>Each hardware function and end-device feature needs to be considered and defined. &#8220;The best techniques for right-sizing AI models for edge AI are being able to categorize your functionality so that you don&#8217;t need a big model,&#8221; said Sathish Balasubramanian, head of product for EDA AI at <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/mentor-a-siemens-business/">Siemens EDA</a>. &#8220;A 4-billion [parameter] model on a sensor is huge, even though we&#8217;re now talking 1 trillion [in the cloud]. Being able to bake that model into less than a billion parameters and have it in each of the sensors is doable. We need to define the highest-priority, must-have function, and train it well in an SLM (small language model). It could even be an ML (machine learning) model or a neural network model. It doesn&#8217;t need to be an LLM.&#8221;</p>
<p>After defining the must-have functions, designers can start building the AI core, then slowly add more features, including must-have, critical, or nice-to-have.</p>
<p>&#8220;Those are the three categories I see people defining in terms of how they do it architectural-wise, and where they fit in what intelligence,&#8221; said Balasubramanian. &#8220;Once you have determined those categories, the must-haves should be very close to the sensor. You can&#8217;t make everything must-have. You really need to prioritize and design it in a way that [the system] is able to be in a self-contained sensor loop with the local compute, local memory requirement, and sometimes even a local backup environment. Everything is cut up. You also need to have at least an hour or two of power envelope available so that the device doesn&#8217;t slow down or shut down.&#8221;</p>
<p>In the automotive sector, companies are increasingly deploying AI models for certain tasks and functions, though much of that industry is still using CNNs from about three or four years ago, Plowman noted. &#8220;They haven&#8217;t fully moved to transformers yet, or embraced the latest compression techniques. But certain external bodies have produced image segmentation object recognition systems, which use transformers.&#8221;</p>
<p>For IP providers tasked with optimizing these models, one challenge is that they may not get to see the actual model their customers are using. &#8220;The automotive guys train their own models, and they&#8217;re very secretive about them, for safety, security, and proprietary IP reasons,&#8221; said Plowman. &#8220;We don&#8217;t get to see them. We get a lot of proxies about what they look like or proxy functions that they want to accelerate, but it&#8217;s very rare that we get an entire model to play with.&#8221;</p>
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" loading="lazy" decoding="async" class="alignnone size-full wp-image-24283643" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/edge2.png?resize=936%2C526&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="936" height="526" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/edge2.png?w=936&amp;ssl=1 936w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/edge2.png?resize=300%2C169&amp;ssl=1 300w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/edge2.png?resize=768%2C432&amp;ssl=1 768w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/edge2.png?resize=600%2C337&amp;ssl=1 600w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 936px) 100vw, 936px" /><br />
<strong>Fig. 2: IP modules for edge AI and physical AI. </strong><a href="https://www.synopsys.com/blogs/chip-design/accelerate-ai-silicon-systems-physical-ai.html">Source</a><strong>: Synopsys</strong></p>
<p>For right-sizing, each IP block needs to be weighed against the model size. &#8220;Right-sizing a model for the edge is a systems problem, not a single-point optimization,&#8221; said Licinio Sousa, senior director of product management at <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/synopsys-inc/">Synopsys</a>. &#8220;Techniques like quantization help the model fit, but real-time latency, power, cost, and functional safety all have to be balanced together. As AI models evolve faster than silicon development cycles, systems also need enough flexibility to support not only today’s workloads, but the models that come next.&#8221;</p>
<p>Once the model and system architecture are aligned, designers must determine which compression techniques can meet those constraints without sacrificing critical accuracy or performance.</p>
<p><strong>Challenges of various compression techniques</strong><br />
Designers typically use a variety of compression techniques to right-size a model, and each has pros and cons.</p>
<p>For example, distillation enables drastic shrinking but potentially more loss. &#8220;You interrogate the model to find and see which bits of it light up,&#8221; said Imagination&#8217;s Plowman. &#8220;You do a similar exercise where you trim things off, but there is always loss from the teacher-student model. The student is trying to refine from the big model the essentials of what the teacher model knows. That&#8217;s why there&#8217;s potentially more inherent loss, but you retain much more of the reasoning capability. It drastically shrinks the size of the model, but there&#8217;s only so far you can go with that without going to quantization.&#8221;</p>
<p>Quantization makes models take a roundabout path to their destination instead of a direct one. &#8220;When applying an input to a network, there is a starting point for navigation,&#8221; said Plowman. &#8220;Then, depending on the values that you&#8217;ve given it, you need to navigate to an endpoint. Every time you do a layer you&#8217;re nudging the transform in a slightly different direction. With a full-width value such as FP32 or FP16, you&#8217;re going much more directly to a given point.&#8221;</p>
<p>Full-width values travel as the crow flies, while quantization is closer to city-block navigation. &#8220;We go up one, over one, up one, over one, but eventually you end up in exactly the same place,&#8221; said Plowman. &#8220;The trick with quantization is making those small, more angular steps mean the same thing as the direct navigation, but without the overhead power, memory, and other costs of the associated mathematics. It starts off with all the weights in the model, and it starts fitting them into buckets that match the quantization. There are lots and lots of different ways of doing the quantization. That&#8217;s a very brutal way of doing it.&#8221;</p>
<p>A gentler way is self-compression. &#8220;Self-compression techniques understand that, as a model is being trained, certain pathways will be very popular while others will be less popular,&#8221; explained Plowman. &#8220;You never know what this dimensional shape will look like. It&#8217;s random every time you retrain. You can maneuver the space to recreate certain desirable shapes you want every time, or get close to them, but there is an element of randomness. You get certain pathways that become critical as scaffolding for the rest of the system, but they&#8217;re not pathways that will matter in the end. We look for the sensitivity to those pathways, then quantize and quantize and quantize them down to zero bits. If nothing changes in the model and you still get the same outcomes, there was likely very small, or infinitesimally small, or no contribution from [those pathways].&#8221;</p>
<p><a href="https://semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/edge3.svg"><img decoding="async" class="alignnone size-full wp-image-24283644" src="https://semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/edge3.svg" alt="" /></a><br />
<strong>Fig. 3: A quantized transformer architecture. </strong><a href="https://blog.imaginationtech.com/shrinking-llms-with-self-compression">Source</a><strong>: Imagination</strong></p>
<p>For quantization and self-compression, the complexity lies in managing the loss. &#8220;You need to make sure you&#8217;re not throwing away things that you really need, and that you&#8217;ve got good data coverage in the training set for the quantization flow,&#8221; Plowman observed.</p>
<p>For example, in automotive, engineers need to avoid losing long-tail data on edge cases or rare training samples that let an autonomous system navigate unusual road conditions.</p>
<p><strong>Conclusion: Trending smaller</strong><br />
Small language models and other compact models suit resource-constrained edge devices, but they require system-level design and optimization to deliver the functions and features a specific product needs.</p>
<p>Ultimately, successful edge AI deployment starts with defining the workload and choosing an appropriately sized architecture — not simply shrinking the largest available model. Compression can help close the remaining gap, but its value depends on preserving accuracy while meeting the device’s power, memory, latency, cost, and safety requirements. As models and hardware evolve at different rates, flexible system design will be essential to support current applications and future workloads.</p>
<p><strong>References</strong></p>
<ol>
<li><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.02234">Structured vs. Unstructured Pruning</a>: An Exponential Gap (Université Côte d’Azur, King’s College London)</li>
<li><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2301.13142">Self-Compressing Neural Networks</a> (Imagination Technologies)</li>
</ol>
<hr />
<p><strong>Related Articles</strong><br />
<a href="https://semiengineering.com/the-hidden-challenges-of-edge-ai-design/">The Hidden Challenges Of Edge AI Design</a><br />
As models evolve faster than silicon cycles, chip architects must balance flexible compute, data movement, and defense-in-depth security.</p>
<p><a href="https://semiengineering.com/can-edge-ai-keep-up/">Can Edge AI Keep Up?</a><br />

		<title>Why Testing Automated Driving Is Harder Than It Looks</title>
		<link>https://semiengineering.com/why-testing-automated-driving-is-harder-than-it-looks/</link>
					<comments>https://semiengineering.com/why-testing-automated-driving-is-harder-than-it-looks/#respond</comments>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Michael Tran]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Thu, 05 Nov 2026 08:02:40 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Blogs - Security]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Design Bytes]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[IoT and Security]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[ADAS]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[automated driving]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[automotive]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[autonomous driving]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Cadence]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[road testing]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">https://semiengineering.com/?p=24283728</guid>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>Instead of waiting for rare situations to occur on public roads, engineers can create those situations virtually. </p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/why-testing-automated-driving-is-harder-than-it-looks/">Why Testing Automated Driving Is Harder Than It Looks</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>Most people assume the hardest part of automated driving is building the technology itself. After all, enabling a vehicle to recognize traffic signs, detect pedestrians, stay within its lane, and react to complex traffic situations sounds like an extraordinary engineering challenge.</p>
<p>It is.</p>
<p>But many automotive engineers would argue that the bigger challenge comes afterward. How do you prove the system works safely?</p>
<p>As vehicles become increasingly software-defined, validation has become one of the most important and demanding aspects of ADAS development. ADAS validation is the process of evaluating whether driver assistance functions behave reliably across relevant driving scenarios, environmental conditions, system configurations, and software versions. The challenge is no longer simply creating intelligence. It is demonstrating that intelligence performs reliably across an almost limitless variety of situations.</p>
<h2>A successful test drive isn&#8217;t enough</h2>
<p>Imagine a new ADAS function successfully navigating a busy urban environment. The vehicle detects traffic signs, maintains safe distances, and responds appropriately to nearby vehicles and pedestrians. Does that mean the system is ready?</p>
<p>Not necessarily. A single test drive represents only one combination of conditions:</p>
<ul>
<li>One road</li>
<li>One traffic pattern</li>
<li>One weather condition</li>
<li>One lighting situation</li>
<li>One vehicle configuration</li>
<li>One software version</li>
</ul>
<p>The next drive could look entirely different:</p>
<ul>
<li>A child might run into the street.</li>
<li>Rain might reduce visibility.</li>
<li>Road markings could be partially faded.</li>
<li>Vehicles could behave unexpectedly.</li>
</ul>
<p>The real challenge is not proving a system works once. It is proving it works consistently across countless different situations.</p>
<h2>The scenario problem</h2>
<p>In ADAS validation, a scenario represents a driving situation defined by elements such as road layout, traffic participants, vehicle behavior, weather, lighting, and sensor conditions. Changing just one variable creates a new scenario.</p>
<p>Now multiply those variables across the thousands of roads, weather conditions, traffic situations, and vehicle configurations an automated driving system may encounter. The result is what many engineers call the scenario explosion problem. There are simply too many possible combinations to evaluate using traditional physical testing alone.</p>
<h2>The real challenge isn&#8217;t creating scenarios. It&#8217;s managing them</h2>
<p>Most organizations no longer struggle to create a handful of test scenarios.</p>
<p>The real challenge begins when those scenarios grow into thousands, hundreds of thousands, or even millions of variations.</p>
<p>Modern ADAS teams must answer questions such as:</p>
<ul>
<li>Which scenarios are most safety-critical?</li>
<li>Which combinations have already been tested?</li>
<li>How much scenario coverage is enough?</li>
<li>Which scenarios should be re-executed after a software update?</li>
<li>How can regression campaigns run automatically as development progresses?</li>
</ul>
<p>As a result, validation is becoming less about individual test cases and more about managing scenario portfolios, measuring coverage, and continuously evaluating software changes.</p>
<p>This is where simulation moves beyond simple virtual driving and becomes part of a broader validation workflow.</p>
<h2>Rare events matter most</h2>
<p>Ironically, some of the most important situations to test are also the least likely to occur.</p>
<p>These are often called edge cases:</p>
<ul>
<li>A pedestrian suddenly crossing between parked vehicles</li>
<li>A cyclist approaching from an unexpected angle</li>
<li>Temporary construction zones</li>
<li>Unusual traffic behavior</li>
<li>Poor weather combined with low visibility</li>
</ul>
<p>These events may occur infrequently in the real world, but they often represent the greatest risks to safety. Finding enough instances of these situations through road testing alone can take enormous amounts of time and effort. In many cases, engineers need a way to recreate these situations repeatedly and under controlled conditions.</p>
<h2>Why physical testing alone cannot scale</h2>
<p>Road testing remains a critical part of vehicle development. Nothing can completely replace evaluating a vehicle in the real world. However, as ADAS functionality grows, the limitations of physical testing become increasingly apparent:</p>
<ul>
<li>Vehicles need to be driven extensively</li>
<li>Tests are difficult to repeat exactly</li>
<li>Environmental conditions constantly change</li>
<li>Rare events are difficult to encounter on demand</li>
<li>Costs increase significantly with scale</li>
</ul>
<h2>Connecting virtual and physical validation</h2>
<p>The limitations of physical testing do not mean that road and proving-ground testing become less important. Instead, the opportunity lies in connecting them more closely with simulation.</p>
<p>One example comes from Daimler Truck, which has publicly described a validation approach that combines simulation, proving-ground testing, and public-road evaluation for advanced safety assistance systems. <a href="https://www.daimlertruck.com/en/newsroom/pressrelease/daimler-truck-showcases-new-and-enhanced-safety-assistance-systems-for-trucks-and-touring-coaches-53587267">Read the Daimler Truck press release.</a></p>
<p>In this type of workflow, simulation can be used to explore a broader range of situations and identify relevant corner cases before selected scenarios move into controlled physical testing. The physical results can then provide another source of evidence for understanding system behavior.</p>
<p>This reflects an important shift in ADAS validation: virtual and physical testing are not competing approaches. Their value increases when they are connected within the same validation process.</p>
<h2>The value of repeatability</h2>
<p>Connecting virtual and physical validation also highlights another critical requirement: repeatability. Engineers need more than successful results. They need confidence that results can be reproduced. Consider a near-collision event encountered during testing. If the exact same situation can be recreated repeatedly, engineers can:</p>
<ul>
<li>Investigate root causes</li>
<li>Compare alternative software versions</li>
<li>Validate fixes</li>
<li>Measure improvements</li>
<li>Evaluate sensor performance</li>
</ul>
<p>Repeatability transforms testing from observation into engineering. Without it, understanding system behavior becomes significantly more difficult.</p>
<h2>Why simulation is becoming essential</h2>
<p>This is where simulation begins to change the equation. Instead of waiting for rare situations to occur on public roads, engineers can create those situations virtually. A scenario can be repeated hundreds or thousands of times while controlling individual variables:</p>
<ul>
<li>Weather</li>
<li>Traffic density</li>
<li>Vehicle speed</li>
<li>Sensor configurations</li>
<li>Road infrastructure</li>
</ul>
<p>The emerging validation model is therefore not “virtual or physical.” It is a connected loop in which simulation helps identify what should be investigated further, while controlled physical testing provides additional evidence for evaluating system behavior.</p>
<p>Simulation also enables a level of automation that is impossible to achieve through physical testing alone. Modern development teams increasingly integrate virtual validation into continuous development workflows, allowing software updates to trigger automated regression campaigns against predefined scenario libraries. This helps organizations identify issues earlier, evaluate changes more consistently, and accelerate development without sacrificing confidence.</p>
<h2>The road ahead</h2>
<p>As vehicles continue to evolve, validation is becoming a data problem as much as an engineering problem. Future development workflows will increasingly combine:</p>
<ul>
<li>Scenario-based validation</li>
<li><a href="https://www.asam.net/standards/detail/openscenario-xml/">ASAM OpenSCENARIO</a> and <a href="https://www.asam.net/standards/detail/opendrive/">ASAM OpenDRIVE</a> assets</li>
<li>Closed-loop simulation</li>
<li>Sensor and ECU integration</li>
<li>Continuous regression testing</li>
<li>Cloud-scale execution</li>
</ul>
<p>The goal is not simply to run more tests. It is to create validation workflows that continuously measure coverage, identify gaps, and deliver evidence that software behaves safely as systems evolve.</p>
<p>In this future, success will depend not only on building intelligent vehicles, but also on building validation processes that can scale alongside them.</p>
<h2>Key takeaway</h2>
<p>ADAS validation is not about demonstrating that a system works in one situation. It is about building confidence across a broad range of relevant driving scenarios, including rare edge cases that are difficult to reproduce through road testing alone.</p>
<h2>Explore a complete ADAS validation workflow</h2>
<p>Modern ADAS validation requires more than scenario creation. Engineering teams need to manage scenario libraries, integrate existing toolchains, connect sensor and vehicle models, automate regression testing, and scale validation across increasingly complex software releases.</p>
<p>Discover how <a href="https://www.cadence.com/en_US/home/tools/msc-software/virtual-test-drive.html">Cadence VTD</a> supports end-to-end ADAS and autonomous driving validation workflows, from scenario development and sensor simulation to large-scale virtual testing and continuous validation.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/why-testing-automated-driving-is-harder-than-it-looks/">Why Testing Automated Driving Is Harder Than It Looks</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
