		<item>
		<title>Why Integrated Gate Drive Regulation Matters For High Reliability Space GaN Designs</title>
		<link>https://semiengineering.com/why-integrated-gate-drive-regulation-matters-for-high-reliability-space-gan-designs/</link>
					<comments>https://semiengineering.com/why-integrated-gate-drive-regulation-matters-for-high-reliability-space-gan-designs/#respond</comments>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Maya Chou]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Thu, 05 Nov 2026 08:01:35 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Blogs - Security]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[IoT and Security]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Secured Intelligent Mobility]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[gallium nitride]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[GaN]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[high-reliability]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Infineon Technologies]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[LDO]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[low dropout regulator]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[space]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">https://semiengineering.com/?p=24283716</guid>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>Eliminating the need for a separate bias regulator dedicated to the gate driver for a more streamlined power architecture.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/why-integrated-gate-drive-regulation-matters-for-high-reliability-space-gan-designs/">Why Integrated Gate Drive Regulation Matters For High Reliability Space GaN Designs</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>As Gallium Nitride (GaN) technology becomes increasingly prevalent in space power systems, designers are leveraging its inherent capabilities to deliver higher efficiency, faster switching speeds, and greater power density. These advantages help designers support the growing demands of modern satellite platforms.</p>
<p>While GaN devices are central to many modern power architectures, achieving their full efficiency and switching performance depends on the gate driver that controls them. Infineon HiRel’s new, rad hard single-channel gate driver for GaN delivers the precise drive conditions that GaN devices require. Designed specifically for extreme environment applications, the device incorporates an integrated low dropout regulator (LDO). The LDO simplifies gate-drive implementation while providing a tightly regulated voltage optimized for GaN operation, while also reducing the total bill of materials (BOM).</p>
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" loading="lazy" decoding="async" class="alignnone size-full wp-image-24283717" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Infineon_Integrated-Gate-Drive-Reg-Space-GaN-fig1.png?resize=400%2C176&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="400" height="176" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Infineon_Integrated-Gate-Drive-Reg-Space-GaN-fig1.png?w=400&amp;ssl=1 400w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Infineon_Integrated-Gate-Drive-Reg-Space-GaN-fig1.png?resize=300%2C132&amp;ssl=1 300w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 400px) 100vw, 400px" /></p>
<h2>GaN performance starts at the gate driver</h2>
<p>The switching advantages of GaN stem from device characteristics such as low gate charge, reduced output capacitance, and fast transition times. These attributes enable lower switching losses and support operation at higher frequencies than traditional silicon MOSFET (Si) technologies.</p>
<p>Designers must provide stable and controlled gate-drive voltages to achieve optimal switching behavior. Even relatively small variations in gate voltage can affect switching losses, power-stage efficiency, electromagnetic interference (EMI), device stress, and long-term reliability. GaN devices have extremely high slew rates (dv/dt) when switching. As a result, maintaining a stable and well-controlled gate-drive voltage is critical for predictable system performance across operating conditions.</p>
<h2>Improve drive consistency</h2>
<p>Traditionally, designers generate the gate-drive supply rail using external regulators, bias circuits, or dedicated auxiliary power supplies. While effective, these approaches add components, consume board area, and create additional design considerations related to regulation accuracy, startup behavior, and power sequencing. In space systems, every additional component increases design complexity. It can also drive up qualification and testing costs, add weight, and extend development timelines. Infineon HiRel’s new <a href="https://www.infineon.com/assets/row/public/documents/24/49/infineon-infineon-rad-hard-ics-ric70115-datasheet-en-datasheet-en.pdf?intc=202609_am_en_pss.a.hirel&amp;type=communityreferral&amp;intcid=intc0150110001&amp;aid=ai015011">RIC70115 gate driver</a> simplifies this architecture with many integrated features, such as an integrated LDO that generates a tightly regulated 4.8 V gate-drive rail from input voltages ranging from 4.75 V to 15 V.</p>
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" loading="lazy" decoding="async" class="alignnone size-full wp-image-24283718" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Infineon_Integrated-Gate-Drive-Reg-Space-GaN-fig2.png?resize=464%2C142&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="464" height="142" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Infineon_Integrated-Gate-Drive-Reg-Space-GaN-fig2.png?w=464&amp;ssl=1 464w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Infineon_Integrated-Gate-Drive-Reg-Space-GaN-fig2.png?resize=300%2C92&amp;ssl=1 300w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 464px) 100vw, 464px" /></p>
<p>By regulating the gate-drive supply internally, the RIC70115 helps ensure that GaN transistors receive a consistent drive voltage, even as upstream supply rails vary during normal operations. This approach aligns directly with recommendations outlined in the <a href="https://www.infineon.com/assets/row/public/documents/24/42/infineon-ric70115-gate-driver-features-for-reliable-and-efficient-gan-hemt-operation-in-space-applicationnotes-en.pdf?intc=202609_am_en_ps.a.hirel&amp;type=communityreferral&amp;intcid=intc0158270001&amp;aid=ai015827">application note</a>, which emphasizes the importance of maintaining tightly controlled gate-drive conditions to support efficient and reliable GaN HEMT operation in space environments. Even better is that no additional external components are needed to deliver this regulated drive voltage, helping customers meet their SWaP-C needs. For space programs operating under tight development timelines, this reduction in design complexity can support faster qualification efforts and lower implementation risk.</p>
<h2>Reducing design complexity</h2>
<p>Space power systems present unique challenges beyond those encountered in terrestrial applications. Every additional component introduces potential reliability considerations, increases PCB area requirements, and compromises qualification and system-level verification efforts.</p>
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" loading="lazy" decoding="async" class="alignnone size-full wp-image-24283719" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Infineon_Integrated-Gate-Drive-Reg-Space-GaN-fig3.jpg?resize=400%2C207&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="400" height="207" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Infineon_Integrated-Gate-Drive-Reg-Space-GaN-fig3.jpg?w=400&amp;ssl=1 400w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Infineon_Integrated-Gate-Drive-Reg-Space-GaN-fig3.jpg?resize=300%2C155&amp;ssl=1 300w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 400px) 100vw, 400px" /></p>
<p>The integrated LDO within the RIC70115 eliminates the need for a separate bias regulator dedicated to the gate driver. This results in a more streamlined power architecture that supports the industry’s ongoing pursuit of reduced size, weight, and power (SWaP) advantages. By consolidating gate-drive regulation within the controller itself, designers can reduce component count without sacrificing the precision required for high-performance GaN operation.</p>
<h2>Enabling reliable GaN adoption</h2>
<p>As designers transition from Si to GaN, solutions that simplify implementation without sacrificing performance stand out.</p>
<p>The RIC70115 does just that. With high-speed gate driving and integrated voltage regulation, the device helps optimize GaN operation. Rather than developing and validating a separate regulated gate-drive supply, designers can rely on the device to provide a controlled and consistent bias environment directly within the gate-driver architecture. The integrated approach helps reduce design complexity while enabling the efficiency, switching speed, and power density advantages that drive GaN adoption in next-generation satellite power systems.</p>
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" loading="lazy" decoding="async" class="alignnone size-full wp-image-24283720" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Infineon_Integrated-Gate-Drive-Reg-Space-GaN-fig4.jpg?resize=530%2C354&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="530" height="354" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Infineon_Integrated-Gate-Drive-Reg-Space-GaN-fig4.jpg?w=530&amp;ssl=1 530w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Infineon_Integrated-Gate-Drive-Reg-Space-GaN-fig4.jpg?resize=300%2C200&amp;ssl=1 300w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 530px) 100vw, 530px" /></p>
<p>Evaluate RIC70115 with Infineon HiRel’s companion evaluation board, the RIC70115EVAL1. Prepopulated with two RIC70115 gate driver ICs and two<a href="https://www.infineon.com/products/power/gallium-nitride/gallium-nitride-transistor?intc=202609_am_en_ps.a.hirel&amp;type=communityreferral&amp;intcid=intc0160570001&amp;aid=ai016057"> IG1NT052N10R GaN HEMTs</a> in a half-bridge configuration, the evaluation board highlights RIC70115’s ability to operate in both high- and low-side configurations.</p>
<h2>Conclusion</h2>
<p>When paired with the right gate driver, GaN can enable a more power-dense spacecraft power architecture. Through its integrated LDO, the RIC70115 helps designers reduce complexity, streamline development, and take full advantage of GaN’s efficiency and performance benefits. Take the next step in next-generation space exploration: download the <a href="https://www.infineon.com/assets/row/public/documents/24/49/infineon-infineon-rad-hard-ics-ric70115-datasheet-en-datasheet-en.pdf?intc=202609_am_en_pss.a.hirel&amp;type=communityreferral&amp;intcid=intc0150110001&amp;aid=ai015011">RIC70115 datasheet</a> and the <a href="https://www.infineon.com/assets/row/public/documents/24/42/infineon-ric70115-gate-driver-features-for-reliable-and-efficient-gan-hemt-operation-in-space-applicationnotes-en.pdf?intc=202609_am_en_ps.a.hirel&amp;type=communityreferral&amp;intcid=intc0158270001&amp;aid=ai015827">application note</a> to learn more.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/why-integrated-gate-drive-regulation-matters-for-high-reliability-space-gan-designs/">Why Integrated Gate Drive Regulation Matters For High Reliability Space GaN Designs</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></content:encoded>

		<title>Physical AI Pushes The Limits Of Hardware</title>
		<link>https://semiengineering.com/physical-ai-pushes-the-limits-of-hardware/</link>
					<comments>https://semiengineering.com/physical-ai-pushes-the-limits-of-hardware/#respond</comments>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Brendan Heffernan]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Thu, 05 Nov 2026 08:01:19 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[IoT and Security]]></category>

		<guid isPermaLink="false">https://semiengineering.com/?p=24283605</guid>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>Autonomous machines promise to transform industries, but memory bottlenecks, power constraints, specialized architectures, and safety requirements are forcing chip designers to rethink the entire system.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/physical-ai-pushes-the-limits-of-hardware/">Physical AI Pushes The Limits Of Hardware</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p><strong>Key Takeaways:</strong></p>
<ul>
<li>More compute alone won’t solve physical AI’s bottlenecks. Processing capability must be matched by memory bandwidth, efficient interfaces, sufficient power, and heat removal.</li>
<li>Physical AI needs system architectures tailored to its end function and environment.</li>
<li>Real-world deployment depends on rigorous validation that covers corner cases, understands behavior, and verifies safety.</li>
</ul>
<hr />
<p>The race is on to develop physical AI fit for widespread use, but building these sensor-powered systems will require engineering teams to address a variety of technical challenges concurrently and continuously.</p>
<p>Analysts predict significant market growth for physical AI, with the promise that autonomous systems will fundamentally reshape industries spanning transportation, manufacturing, medicine, energy, and more. PwC&#8217;s Strategy&amp; projects that the global physical AI market could reach approximately <a href="https://www.strategyand.pwc.com/de/en/industries/telecommunication-media-and-technology/physical-ai.html">€430 billion by 2030</a>, reflecting the potential commercial impact of autonomous systems across multiple industries.</p>
<p>“Physical AI systems are expanding beyond automotive to robotics and drones, and the bottlenecks for development of these applications cover the entire system,&#8221; said Licinio Sousa, senior director of Interface IP for Automotive and Physical AI at <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/synopsys-inc/">Synopsys</a>. &#8220;AI is expanding from the data center to the edge, and with machines such as robots, drones, and vehicles, all driven by the need for autonomy, privacy, personalization, faster response, and less reliance on the cloud.”</p>
<p>The list of technical challenges that need to be addressed is long and intertwined. Memory capacity and bandwidth need to be increased, latency and power consumption need to be decreased, product lifespans need to be stretched, and safety needs to be verified. And if that isn&#8217;t hard enough, addressing any one of these can create problems elsewhere.</p>
<p><strong>Specialized architectures<br />
</strong>Solving these system-wide bottlenecks requires highly specialized hardware and software that are optimized for specific use cases.</p>
<p>“We need to start talking about optimized, more tailored compute that you build for the specific workload, and this is where physical AI gets more interesting,” said Michal Siwinski, executive vice president and chief product and marketing officer, and general manager for Security Solutions at <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/arterisip/">Arteris</a>. “That workload needs to be highly optimized for the hardware. We&#8217;re basically going back to ASICs in the traditional sense of what ASICs were supposed to be — very application-specific, extremely optimized, versus more generic. Some of that is already starting. It will be interesting to see how the more nimble systems evolve for very specific applications between the software and the hardware and the workload, and keep evolving because the models are moving way faster.”</p>
<p>Autonomous robotics systems share some of the same technology found in self-driving vehicles, particularly in perception and control, but each physical AI use case adds unique challenges.</p>
<p>“Robotics is not a new AI stack,” Vasanthan Karunanithy, staff systems architect for Automotive at Micron Technology, said at the recent JEDEC Automotive Forum. “From an ADAS system, the robot inherits real-time perception, path planning, and motion control. From an intelligent cabinet, robotics inherits natural language understanding, contextual reasoning, and personal interaction. In embodied intelligence, the bigger challenge is running these two workloads together and then exchanging information at the end. So that is the commonality between these two segments. But there is also a difference that tells the scale of the channel. An autonomous vehicle operates in a relatively structured road environment and draws power from a larger source — the vehicle battery. But when you look at robots, they operate in an unstructured 3D environment. [A robot] also needs to maintain multiple degrees of freedom. It has to manipulate objects and maintain balance, and it has to understand the commands in the instructions. It has to do all this with a limited power source compared to automotive.”</p>
<p>Those distinctions vary considerably across applications. Autonomous vehicles operate within established transportation infrastructure, but they must respond to unpredictable road conditions. Mobile and humanoid robots, in contrast, may need to navigate less structured environments, coordinate manipulation and balance, and operate within much more restrictive power budgets. Meanwhile, industrial robots may operate in controlled environments with fixed electrical power. These differences make it hard to optimize a single architecture for every form of physical AI.</p>
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" loading="lazy" decoding="async" class="aligncenter wp-image-24283610 size-full" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Synopsys-Phy.-AI-Architecture-.png?resize=1200%2C675&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="1200" height="675" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Synopsys-Phy.-AI-Architecture-.png?w=1200&amp;ssl=1 1200w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Synopsys-Phy.-AI-Architecture-.png?resize=300%2C169&amp;ssl=1 300w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Synopsys-Phy.-AI-Architecture-.png?resize=1024%2C576&amp;ssl=1 1024w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Synopsys-Phy.-AI-Architecture-.png?resize=768%2C432&amp;ssl=1 768w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Synopsys-Phy.-AI-Architecture-.png?resize=600%2C338&amp;ssl=1 600w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 1000px) 100vw, 1000px" /><br />
<strong>Fig. 1: Robotic system. Source: Synopsys.</strong></p>
<p>Designers must build system architecture with these requirements in mind from the start.</p>
<p>“As workloads and models evolve, we are already seeing purpose-built silicon designed to meet the specific needs of each class of machine,” Sousa said. “Our customers continue to address the need for advanced VLA (vision-language-action) models. In the hardware world, this translates to more intensive compute and AI engines. At the same time, the spectrum of use cases makes it harder for a single monolithic architecture, leading to a transition to chiplets where compute, AI, memory, and I/Os can be scaled using a UCIe interface based on specific use cases.”</p>
<p>Chiplets offer one potential path to scalable physical AI platforms, particularly when heterogeneous compute, memory, and I/O requirements justify advanced packaging. However, they also can ratchet up costs and complexity due to customized packaging, thermal management, and die-to-die communication. Monolithic SoCs may be preferable for smaller, highly integrated systems where power, size, and cost are constrained.</p>
<p>Those constraints apply to both hardware and software. “The sensors are going to be much more focused on use cases, and you need to make sure that you have good use cases, and it&#8217;s customized, and you need to have a software layer on top of it as well,” said Sathish Balasubramanian, head of product for EDA AI &amp; Solido at <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/mentor-a-siemens-business/">Siemens EDA</a>. “I always joke that everything involved in the real world is analog and we are trying to abstract it to digital, so one of the most important things is redundancy. Sensors have gotten pretty good. We can sense in terms of visual, thermal, pressure, anything. We have gotten good in terms of radar and everything else. What&#8217;s more important is making sure that the sensors can operate in the environment, and that environment needs to be captured in a way that it doesn&#8217;t make it doesn&#8217;t really degrade, or you have redundancy built in.”</p>
<p>Still, the effectiveness of redundancy can vary, depending on whether components can fail independently and whether the system can identify and respond to faults. Adding sensors alone doesn&#8217;t guarantee reliable operation, especially when environmental conditions can affect multiple sensors at once.</p>
<p>Tailoring the compute architecture to the workload or use case introduces tradeoffs, as well. “There is the question of how close the intelligence needs to be to the actual origination of where the sensing is happening, and there&#8217;s a push-pull,” said Yasmine King, corporate vice president and head of Automotive at Analog Devices. “If you have intelligence distributed throughout a system, that makes it more complicated to update. Centralizing allows one point of over-the-air updates, easy to patch and fix, but it can create latency issues when you need to react quickly to this data. In a vehicle, if a pedestrian runs out in front of your car, you need to be able to respond extremely quickly. So where you locate your intelligence is based on what sensory information you&#8217;re trying to gather, and how quickly you need to respond.”</p>
<p><a href="https://www.linkedin.com/company/reuterseventsautomotive/?originalSubdomain=uk"><img data-recalc-dims="1" loading="lazy" decoding="async" class="alignnone wp-image-24283616 size-full" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/AUTOMOTIVE_USA_2026_000513-scaled.jpg?resize=2560%2C1707&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="2560" height="1707" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/AUTOMOTIVE_USA_2026_000513-scaled.jpg?w=2560&amp;ssl=1 2560w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/AUTOMOTIVE_USA_2026_000513-scaled.jpg?resize=300%2C200&amp;ssl=1 300w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/AUTOMOTIVE_USA_2026_000513-scaled.jpg?resize=1024%2C683&amp;ssl=1 1024w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/AUTOMOTIVE_USA_2026_000513-scaled.jpg?resize=768%2C512&amp;ssl=1 768w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/AUTOMOTIVE_USA_2026_000513-scaled.jpg?resize=1536%2C1024&amp;ssl=1 1536w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/AUTOMOTIVE_USA_2026_000513-scaled.jpg?resize=2048%2C1365&amp;ssl=1 2048w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/AUTOMOTIVE_USA_2026_000513-scaled.jpg?resize=600%2C400&amp;ssl=1 600w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 1000px) 100vw, 1000px" /></a><br />
<strong>Fig. 2 Analog Devices&#8217; Yasmine King speaks at Reuters Automotive 2026 Oct. 27, 2026. Source: Semiconductor Engineering</strong></p>
<p>The placement of intelligence is an architectural decision involving more than compute performance. It requires balancing response time, communication bandwidth, power consumption, updateability, as well as coordination of distributed components. Centralized compute may simplify management, while local processing can reduce communication delays and preserve essential functionality when connectivity is interrupted.</p>
<p><strong>Physical AI’s memory wall<br />
</strong>Memory capacity, bandwidth, latency, and energy consumption stand out as key chokepoints for physical AI, alongside potential supply constraints for some memory technologies. The challenge is designing a memory subsystem capable of feeding increasingly demanding AI workloads within tight power and thermal limits, not just adding a sufficient number of memory components.</p>
<p>“The billions of associated model parameters also need to be stored and transported across storage, memory, and processing elements,” Sousa said. “Today, this is referred to as the memory wall. As memory footprint continues to grow, DRAM needs an ultra-fast interface, such as LPDDR6, to no longer be a bottleneck to processing.”</p>
<p>Performance also depends on access patterns, latency, data reuse, cache capacity, memory-controller efficiency, and the energy required to move data. Increasing raw bandwidth may provide limited benefit if workloads are constrained by other parts of the memory hierarchy.</p>
<p>Workload-specific system design can help offset some memory burdens. “Coming up with right-sized memory, right-sized compute and redundancy, everything that needs to fit into your thermal and power envelope is going to be very key,” said Balasubramanian. “The most important thing is that size matters. You don&#8217;t need a 100-gallon tank in your Prius. You need a 10-gallon tank. That&#8217;s the analogy I use in terms of memory. You can manage it in a way that you don&#8217;t need a huge DRAM, but you have a pretty good cache and an SRAM built in. It can always store critical requirements in DRAM, but you can move everything else to flash or SRAM when needed. The memory path, in terms of cache coherency from DRAM to SRAM, to the actual compute, and to making the actual physical AI, is going to be very key.”</p>
<p>The trade-offs between these memory technologies are significant. On-chip SRAM and caches can provide fast access to frequently used data, but their capacity is limited by silicon area and cost. External DRAM offers greater capacity, while flash provides nonvolatile storage for models, software, and other information that doesn&#8217;t need to remain in active memory.</p>
<p>Moving information among these tiers can reduce some capacity requirements, but it adds transfer overhead and latency. Cache coherency, meanwhile, focuses on keeping copies of data consistent across caches and memory, rather than simply moving information between storage technologies, but that adds more overhead. So engineers must consider the working set, data locality, and access frequency when determining how much memory belongs at each level.</p>
<p>Automotive memory innovations will likely loom large in how the issue is addressed for physical AI.</p>
<p>“It’s very closely coupled to our automotive range at the moment, and we see the majority of requirements from the robotics side coming directly from the automotive side,” said Rob Fisher, senior director of product management at <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/imagination-technologies/">Imagination Technologies</a>. “A lot of automotive players will now present their chips as automotive and robotics platforms, so the 4-, 6-, and 8-bit support are really going to benefit memory bandwidth and memory footprint of embedded edge-type applications. It&#8217;s less of a concern in data centers, but the impact is different. You can probably have a bigger model in a data center by using a smaller bit width on your words. In an automotive application and a robotics application, you&#8217;ll probably take the other side of that benefit, which is a smaller memory footprint. You&#8217;ll constrain your model to do exactly what you need it to do, and then you&#8217;ll reduce the amount of memory and bandwidth you have available to contain it.”</p>
<p>Lower-precision representations can reduce model storage and memory bandwidth requirements, making them particularly attractive for embedded AI. But the benefits depend on whether the accelerator efficiently supports the chosen numerical format and whether quantization preserves the application&#8217;s required accuracy. Formats such as 6-bit representations can also introduce packing or implementation overhead, limiting the gains achievable on hardware not designed for them.</p>
<p>Flash storage also plays a critical role in managing memory for autonomous systems.</p>
<p>“DRAM is constrained in capacity, power, and availability,” Sousa said. “Faster storage bandwidth shortens load time, so the same DRAM budget can support larger models. These systems run continuously on batteries and often without active cooling, so every data transfer costs power and adds delay, making it necessary for each component — mechanical, electronic, and software — to limit power consumption and improve efficiency. Interfaces such as MIPI, UFS, LPDDR, PCIe, etc., have techniques built in to ensure fast transmission of bursty data and then transitioning to long low-power idle modes.”</p>
<p>Faster non-volatile storage can shorten model-loading delays and, when paired with appropriate model partitioning or weight-streaming techniques, allow systems with limited DRAM to execute workloads whose total model footprint exceeds available active memory. However, storage bandwidth alone does not increase the amount of data that can be kept in DRAM at any point in time.</p>
<p>The interfaces used to move that data also differ in their power-management mechanisms and operating characteristics. Their efficiency depends on the particular specification, implementation, transfer patterns, and opportunities to enter low-power states.</p>
<p>Physical AI’s memory demands could eventually justify new memory technologies or variants of existing ones, provided the market becomes large enough to support the investment.</p>
<p>“Memory is a critical technology, and it&#8217;s going to be needed in all the physical AI that&#8217;s out there,” said Steven Woo, fellow and distinguished inventor at <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/rambus-inc/">Rambus</a>. “What&#8217;s different about these markets is some of them are going to just leverage the memories that are out there, but it&#8217;s possible some of them could become so big in terms of market sizes that they may warrant their own type of memory or some variant of an existing memory. We saw that with HBM, for example. People were using other kinds of memories, and suddenly the market got big. And if you go back before LPDDR, phones were just using binned, carefully selected DDR parts that just happened to be low power. The market got big enough, and people said, ‘Well, let&#8217;s just make a special part.’ Physical AI has the potential to become a big enough market on its own, so that if the requirements vary enough, there could be another kind of memory.”</p>
<p>Whether such specialization occurs will depend on the scale and consistency of demand. Unlike data centers, physical AI spans applications with widely differing requirements for cost, capacity, endurance, bandwidth, temperature, and energy efficiency. That diversity may favor adaptations of existing memory technologies in some markets and purpose-built solutions in others.</p>
<p><strong>Validating the edge cases<br />
</strong>Ensuring a system behaves safely, including under rare and unexpected conditions, is another major challenge. Solving the validation problem is especially important as physical AI developers target integration into heavy machinery, medical devices, and other safety-critical technologies.</p>
<p>“The validation loop that we need to bring physical AI into before we get into any production is paramount, and I would say is the most important thing we need to do,” said Siemens&#8217; Balasubramanian. “What happens if there is a huge power cut or there&#8217;s an earthquake, and your fiber optic cable is cut from the outside world, or there’s a power cut? How do you manage that kind of a scenario on the edge? Having some kind of compute, some kind of memory, and some kind of intelligence close to the sensor is going to be very key, and the way people do that is by coming up with what they determine is the most important thing given use-case needs. In an industrial environment, when do I need to stop the assembly line? When do I need to know that I need to phone back or start the siren or anything else? Those are some of the things that need to be handled within a given sensor.”</p>
<p>These scenarios illustrate why safety-critical functions cannot always depend on continuous cloud connectivity or centralized decision-making. Local processing may be necessary to recognize faults, trigger protective actions, or place equipment into a defined safe state when communications or power systems fail.</p>
<p>But verification and validation address different parts of the problem. Verification establishes whether a design satisfies specified requirements, while validation examines whether the system behaves appropriately in its intended operating environment. Safety assurance may additionally require hazard analysis, fault-injection testing, evaluation of common-cause failures, and evidence of compliance with applicable standards.</p>
<p>Systems must therefore be designed to establish and verify trust throughout the relevant hardware and software stack, with assurance requirements matched to each use case.</p>
<p>“When we&#8217;re designing something for a wheelchair, we have specific needs, like any physical AI application,” said Owen Kent, co-founder of assistive robotics startup ATDev. “You have to be able to really put this hardware through the wringer in your quality management system and your third-party electromagnetic compatibility testing. Whatever hardware you use, whether it&#8217;s custom or not, you have to be able to verify safety.”</p>
<p>For developers, that means evaluating whether components function correctly in normal operation, as well as how the complete system responds to degraded sensors, communication failures, unexpected inputs, and other foreseeable hazards. Passing component-level tests is necessary in many applications, but it does not by itself establish system-level safety.</p>
<p><strong>Conclusion<br />
</strong>Physical AI’s practical limits extend across the entire path from sensing to action. Designers must match models and hardware to the task, move data within available energy and thermal budgets, and validate how machines behave when conditions or connections change. Increasing compute capacity only helps when the rest of the system can support it.</p>
<p>“Start with standards-based, low-power and proven interfaces along the whole path, from sensor to processor to actuator,” said Balasubramanian. “They give designers predictable behavior, ecosystem interoperability, and silicon-proven building blocks.”</p>
<p>A relevant example is the MIPI Camera Security Framework, which combines the Camera Service Extensions (CSE) 2.0 specification with complementary security specifications. CSE 2.0 supports data-integrity protection and optional encryption of CSI-2 camera data, while the broader framework addresses authentication of imaging-system components and secure-session establishment.</p>
<p>“It provides end-to-end security for camera data, covering integrity, authentication, and confidentiality from the image sensor to the SoC,” he said. “It also gives designers flexible options to minimize compute and traffic overhead, power, and latency, so security can be matched to the application rather than designed for the worst case.”</p>
<p>The distinction matters because confidentiality is optional, and the level of protection depends on the security services and configurations implemented. The broader framework lets developers balance protection against processing overhead, power consumption, and latency, rather than applying identical protections to every data stream.</p>
<p>It&#8217;s not enough to make autonomous machines more capable. They also must be dependable within real-world constraints. For physical AI, the architecture choices that conserve memory, power, and bandwidth must also support predictable behavior, resilience, and the safety assurance that widespread deployment demands.</p>
<hr />
<p><strong>Related Articles<br />
</strong><a href="https://semiengineering.com/why-physical-ai-is-so-hard-to-engineer/">Why Physical AI Is So Hard To Engineer<br />
</a>Integrating sensors, compute, and actuators into autonomous systems adds new challenges for chip design.</p>
<p><a href="https://semiengineering.com/scaling-data-movement-for-physical-ai/">Scaling Data Movement For Physical AI<br />
</a>One size does not fit all.</p>
<p><a href="https://semiengineering.com/redefining-roles-for-edge-and-cloud-ai/">Redefining Roles For Edge And Cloud AI<br />
</a>The race is on for localized intelligence.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/physical-ai-pushes-the-limits-of-hardware/">Physical AI Pushes The Limits Of Hardware</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></content:encoded>

		<title>College Grads And AI</title>
		<link>https://semiengineering.com/college-grads-and-ai/</link>
					<comments>https://semiengineering.com/college-grads-and-ai/#respond</comments>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Brian Bailey]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Thu, 05 Nov 2026 08:01:03 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[IoT and Security]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Round Tables]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Top Stories]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[abstraction]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[AI]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[apprentice]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Breker Verification Systems]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Cadence]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[ChipAgents]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[computer science]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[design]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[disruption]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[EDA]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[education]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[employment]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[formal]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[graduates]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[internships]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[llmda.ai]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[productivity]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[semiconductor]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[skills]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[software]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Southampton University]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[students]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Synopsys]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[verification]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">https://semiengineering.com/?p=24283565</guid>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>Computer science majors may be in an awkward space; EE grads are fine for now.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/college-grads-and-ai/">College Grads And AI</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p><strong>Key takeaways</strong>:</p>
<ul>
<li>There is no drop in demand for electrical engineering graduates, but the demands on their education and work experience are changing.</li>
<li>Agents are now doing many tasks that were considered the work of junior engineers, but there are new higher-level tasks for them to do.</li>
<li>There is an opportunity for junior engineers to help retrain senior engineers in the use of AI.</li>
</ul>
<hr />
<p><strong>Experts at the Table</strong>: Semiconductor Engineering sat down to discuss what is happening with the latest college graduates in the face of AI adoption, with Chuck Alpert, fellow at <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/cadence-design-systems/">Cadence</a>; Thomas Andersen, vice president of AI and ML at <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/synopsys-inc/">Synopsys</a>; Simon Davidmann, AI and EDA researcher at Southampton University; Nagesh Gupta, CEO of llmda.ai; Dave Kelf, CEO of Breker Verification Systems; and Kexun Zhang, Head of Research from <a href="https://semiengineering.com/entities/alpha-design-ai-chipagents/">ChipAgents</a>. What follows are excerpts of that discussion. This is the first article in a three-part series.</p>
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" loading="lazy" decoding="async" class="alignnone size-large wp-image-24283567" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/College-Grads-AI-roundtable.png?resize=1024%2C389&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="1024" height="389" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/College-Grads-AI-roundtable.png?resize=1024%2C389&amp;ssl=1 1024w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/College-Grads-AI-roundtable.png?resize=300%2C114&amp;ssl=1 300w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/College-Grads-AI-roundtable.png?resize=768%2C292&amp;ssl=1 768w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/College-Grads-AI-roundtable.png?resize=600%2C228&amp;ssl=1 600w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/College-Grads-AI-roundtable.png?w=1430&amp;ssl=1 1430w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 1000px) 100vw, 1000px" /></p>
<p><strong>Clockwise from top left: Southampton University&#8217;s Davidmann; Breker&#8217;s Kelf; Synopsys&#8217; Andersen; ChipAgents&#8217; Kexun; llmda&#8217;s Gupta; and Cadence&#8217;s Alpert.</strong></p>
<p><strong>SE: AI is disruptive to EDA, the semiconductor industry, and to their customers. One early indication is that computer science (CS) grads are struggling to find jobs. In the past, they were highly sought after. Today, they have moved to a higher unemployability rate than the general averages. What&#8217;s going on?</strong></p>
<p><strong>Gupta</strong>: From my observations, data science is much more significantly affected compared to computer science. I&#8217;ve been looking for computer science grads, but from good universities. There are not that many good CS grads available. It&#8217;s more of the data science that seems to be affected.</p>
<p><strong>Alpert</strong>: We know that the vibe-coding tools — Cursor, Claude Code, GitHub Copilot — are improving the productivity of computer scientists, and so it&#8217;s natural there will be a hiring slowdown. Companies are spending a lot of money on Claude and OpenAI for these things, and they don&#8217;t want to hire quite so many engineers at quite the same rate.</p>
<p><strong>Andersen</strong>: When we talk about computer scientists, or folks in our industry, there are two aspects to consider. There are folks that write EDA software, and then there are chip design companies. I would agree that when it comes to software development, everybody&#8217;s a little cautious because they want to see how much AI can automate things. At the same time, when we work on new technology and new releases, everyone wants to know how you can do it faster. If I can do things faster and more efficiently, if I can make releases every three months and develop software faster, that is definitely beneficial. On the chip designer side, it&#8217;s not quite at the same level because they are not like traditional software programmers. They are primarily experts in chip design, and they are users of tools. There&#8217;s been a shortage of those people for quite some time. To some degree, AI is helping address that shortage, but it&#8217;s not that humans are going away. Humans are just elevating the tasks that they&#8217;re doing, and can be more efficient, and then they can develop more chips. How long does it take to get a chip out the door? If somebody says I can do it in half the time, everybody would love it.</p>
<p><strong>Alpert</strong>: I&#8217;m guessing none of this applies to electrical engineers (EE) because there is still a huge gap in demand for design expertise. I don&#8217;t know if there&#8217;s a slowdown there.</p>
<p><strong>Davidmann</strong>: I did some digging around for the UK market, and they reckon that the graduate market is difficult, but AI is not the only cause. They said that from 2022 to 2026, graduate recruitment among leading employers fell 24%, but that is about economic uncertainty, reduced budgets, corrections, and post-pandemic hiring changes. AI has come into an area that was already complicated. It&#8217;s not easy to work out what effect AI is having. From an engineering point of view, they said that graduate engineers are finding employment within 15 months of graduating. So 75% of them got jobs, and that&#8217;s pretty typical. It is definitely having some effect, but it&#8217;s very hard to pinpoint what the actual AI impact is on changing employment because there are so many other things going on.</p>
<p><strong>Kelf</strong>: I ran AI to ask this question and got similar answers. I was talking to someone who&#8217;s leaving college and trying to find a job. They can&#8217;t find one. The problem is a lack of practical experience, and we&#8217;ve all seen this over the years. We&#8217;re trying to hire people, and if you hire a new grad, you&#8217;ve got to spend a year to get them up to speed on real designs. That seems to be getting worse. Something we have seen is that AI is somewhat replacing the need for interns, and so finding internships seems to be getting harder, and therefore they&#8217;re getting even less practical experience. The colleges aren&#8217;t really helping with that. When we hire a new grad, we&#8217;ve got to spend even more time training them. There&#8217;s also a clear financial question. If we can put AI on some of the more junior tasks — tasks that you would have used to train new hires — then we can&#8217;t afford to hire them anymore. If we hire them and our competitors are using AI, then we have a problem with profitability versus our competitors. We&#8217;ve got to keep up with those.</p>
<p><strong>Davidmann</strong>: That is a short-term problem for graduates because AI only does certain things. There are many skills that you need to learn and put in practice for real engineering, which AI doesn&#8217;t really help you with. If you don&#8217;t have graduates coming in and apprenticeships, you will have a problem because they never become the experts. And as your experts get old, then you&#8217;ve got this big gap, which AI isn&#8217;t going to fix.</p>
<p><strong>Andersen</strong>: I completely agree. While you can say I don&#8217;t need the junior guys anymore because AI can do those simple tasks, if I don&#8217;t replace the existing experts, who&#8217;s ever going to do it?</p>
<p><strong>Gupta</strong>: From my own experience, my daughter is a recent CS grad, and fortunately she did find a job. I was talking to her about the other CS grads, and she told me pretty much everyone in the graduating class got jobs. But she made a very interesting observation. Previously, if you were a new grad, you would get assigned to a mentor who trained you and got you up to speed. What is happening today is that all the senior folks in the company are using AI to do a lot of their work. The young grads are not able to get time from the experienced folks to train them. This is a big problem, and the large company she is working for realized this. They have started doing a focus group to figure out how to solve this problem, where nobody is interested in taking on the younger grads and getting them up to speed. That seems to be a key problem.</p>
<p><strong>Zhang</strong>: There is a big mismatch between what people expect new grads to do when they join the company in the AI era, compared to how they&#8217;re trained either in schools, universities, or the internship programs. AI is evolving so fast, and we are now expecting the new hires, the new grads to be AI-native, to be able to use AI tools to solve problems at a higher level. We&#8217;re not expecting them to do the traditional grunt work that junior engineers or interns did. We&#8217;re expecting them to solve higher-level problems with taste, with their own initiative for defining problems. Solving these problems and implementing the solution has become much easier, but university education hasn&#8217;t really adapted, and it&#8217;s impossible for it to adapt this fast given the pace of AI and its evolving ability.</p>
<p><strong>Gupta</strong>: This is especially true for the recent college grads, because they&#8217;ve been in this transition.</p>
<p><strong>Zhang</strong>: Universities are trying to adapt, and the recent grads are the generation who are in this gap. Most of their education has not been AI-native, but the companies that are seeing the economic value of using AI have already changed their expectations for new grads.</p>
<p><strong>Davidmann</strong>: Being back in the university scene, there&#8217;s schizophrenia in the universities about what they do with AI, about how much they let people use AI, and what type of assessments they apply. They need to build assessments that can&#8217;t be done by AI, where the engineer, the student, has to have learnt the skills. I have two views on it. Education has to change and separate between two types of teaching, a foundation mode and a professional mode. The professional mode is what you expect them to do in a company. This means using AI, using the agents as part of the job. Then there&#8217;s the foundation mode where you need to know the mathematics of things, how the algorithms work, and how you debug things. There are areas where you shouldn&#8217;t be using AI, because you need the understanding skills. But that is of no use in the industry because you&#8217;re going to be using agents and AI. You really need to have these two things. While working in one of the large EDA companies, I looked after the university program in Europe. One thing we did was to lend them technology for verification and give them a project to do with coverage. First they got to understand the problem, manually got the simulation going, the test generators going, the coverage going. They did all of this manually and did manual tests. They learnt the whole process, and then they tried to use AI to improve it. It was interesting to listen to them present their project. Education has to change with the use of AI. In the same way, companies are learning how to make use of AI and how the staff can use it. The educators, the lecturers, have to understand it, and these policies aren&#8217;t sorted. Similarly, they&#8217;re not in companies. It&#8217;s all changing very quickly.</p>
<p><strong>Alpert</strong>: I&#8217;m finding that a lot of the junior engineers, people who have been hired within the last two years, are some of the best experts to rely on for AI expertise. It&#8217;s really exciting for EDA because we have a lot of mature people. Three to five years ago, if I had asked what a young engineer could teach me, the answer would have been nothing. But today I can. I learn a lot from talking to these guys.</p>
<p><strong>Andersen</strong>: I fully agree with that. With a lot of the established folks, there&#8217;s some resistance to using new technology. That happens with every generation. It&#8217;s also true that maybe there is a gap, in the sense that universities have not prepared the current graduates for these technologies. But in general, younger people are very eager to pick up new stuff. They can be self-learning, and that&#8217;s a big asset.</p>
<p><strong>Kelf</strong>: There have been waves within EDA. Think about logic synthesis. In the past, when synthesis started coming in, senior engineers were working at the gate level. They were so used to that, and they were good at it. They were resistant to picking up synthesis, and it was the new grads and new engineers who figured out HDLs, and they were the ones who started using synthesis. We saw it again when randomized verification was introduced. The senior verification guys weren&#8217;t used to using test benches, which required expertise in object-oriented programming, but the new grads were learning that at school. The question with AI is what they could learn about AI to do these jobs more practically compared to senior engineers who are having to figure this out by themselves. I wonder if there&#8217;s an expectation that the kids coming out of school will be experts in AI because they&#8217;re learning it, but they&#8217;re not there yet. Maybe in another year or two they will start having much more intuitive AI courses, and they&#8217;ll be able to figure things out like hallucinations and other things that the senior engineers will have trouble figuring out. Maybe this is the next wave.</p>
<p><strong>SE: It has been said that this won&#8217;t affect the EEs, that it&#8217;s the CS grads who are impacted. But people within the EDA industry are saying that the current agents are as good as junior engineers. They also say we are going to have senior engineers managing 10 of these agents. They call them virtual engineers rather than people. This says to me that EEs are going to get impacted in the same way.</strong></p>
<p><strong>Alpert</strong>: There&#8217;s such a huge productivity gap, and the designs are more complex. I&#8217;ve been hearing that everything is 10× more complex because we have to do the packaging and 3D-ICs, and we have to verify all these different fields, and we have to do drop tests. There is so much more to do to get things out the door. This is over and above doing 10× more designs with no more resources. The timeframes are the same. EDA has always been about productivity. That&#8217;s what we do as an industry. Now we need to accelerate that with agents, and we can. But that&#8217;s just another productivity gain that&#8217;s going to help close the gap. I still don&#8217;t think it&#8217;s going to be closed. The number of designs people want to do means there&#8217;s never going to be a shortage of EE&#8217;s, even if designers are 10× more productive.</p>
<p><strong>Andersen</strong>: There are many studies that show there is a talent shortage. When it comes to chip designers, there&#8217;s definitely more demand than supply, and that means it would be improved by AI, where people can be more effective. When we have terms like junior versus senior engineer, it simply describes the tasks that they&#8217;re doing. If the current task of a junior engineer is being automated, then they do a higher-level task. That doesn&#8217;t necessarily mean they need to spend 20 years getting experience so that they can do the higher-level tasks. They just need to level up from the beginning.</p>
<p><strong>Alpert</strong>: All engineers are now senior engineers. That&#8217;s what we mean. When you hire someone, you get some help.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/college-grads-and-ai/">College Grads And AI</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></content:encoded>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>Shared resources, a mix of tools running at different speeds, and multiple dies are adding new challenges for design teams.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/accelerating-plug-and-play-prototyping-in-chiplet-based-designs/">Accelerating Plug-And-Play Prototyping In Chiplet-Based Designs</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>Verifying chips is becoming much more difficult in multi-die assemblies. Unlike in the past, when simulation was sufficient, designs now require a combination of simulation, emulation, and FPGA prototyping to ensure that entire systems will work as expected. Purna Mohanty, CEO of SignatureIP, talks about the impact of chiplets and shared memory resources, the challenges of version control across multiple flavors of I/O, and the challenges engineering teams face when hardware-accelerated tools are running at different speeds than the actual design.</p>
<p><iframe loading="lazy" src="https://www.youtube.com/embed/lkPX3lINOX4" width="480" height="270" frameborder="0" allowfullscreen="allowfullscreen"></iframe></p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/accelerating-plug-and-play-prototyping-in-chiplet-based-designs/">Accelerating Plug-And-Play Prototyping In Chiplet-Based Designs</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>

		<title>Adding Network Security to AI, HPC and Data Centers</title>
		<link>https://semiengineering.com/adding-network-security-to-ai-hpc-and-data-centers/</link>
					<comments>https://semiengineering.com/adding-network-security-to-ai-hpc-and-data-centers/#respond</comments>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Rambus]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Wed, 04 Nov 2026 08:04:45 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[IoT and Security]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Whitepapers]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[data center architecture]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[data center networking security]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[data center networks]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[data center security]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[high-performance computing]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[HPC]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[network security]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[network security protocols]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Rambus]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">https://semiengineering.com/?p=24283628</guid>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>How data center operators can protect critical data and communications without compromising the performance demanded by distributed AI and HPC workloads.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://semiengineering.com/adding-network-security-to-ai-hpc-and-data-centers/">Adding Network Security to AI, HPC and Data Centers</a> appeared first on <a href="https://semiengineering.com">Semiconductor Engineering</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>AI and HPC workloads are transforming data center networking by creating distinct front-end and back-end network domains, each with different performance, scalability, and operational requirements. While front-end networks connect users, applications, storage, and management systems, back-end networks provide the high-speed connectivity required among GPUs, accelerators, and compute nodes. This white paper examines the architectural and security considerations associated with these specialized networks and explains how data center operators can protect critical data and communications without compromising the performance demanded by distributed AI and HPC workloads.</p>
<p>Learn:</p>
<ul>
<li>How front-end and back-end networks serve different roles in modern AI and HPC infrastructure</li>
<li>The performance, operational, and security requirements of each network domain</li>
<li>How to protect data and communications across increasingly distributed compute environments</li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Read <a href="https://go.rambus.com/thank-you-adding-network-security-to-ai-hpc-and-data-centers">more here</a>.</strong></p>
<p><img data-recalc-dims="1" loading="lazy" decoding="async" class="alignnone wp-image-24283629" src="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-03-at-9.28.28-AM.png?resize=582%2C194&#038;ssl=1" alt="" width="582" height="194" srcset="https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-03-at-9.28.28-AM.png?resize=300%2C100&amp;ssl=1 300w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-03-at-9.28.28-AM.png?resize=1024%2C340&amp;ssl=1 1024w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-03-at-9.28.28-AM.png?resize=768%2C255&amp;ssl=1 768w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-03-at-9.28.28-AM.png?resize=1536%2C510&amp;ssl=1 1536w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-03-at-9.28.28-AM.png?resize=600%2C199&amp;ssl=1 600w, https://i0.wp.com/semiengineering.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/Screenshot-2026-11-03-at-9.28.28-AM.png?w=1626&amp;ssl=1 1626w" sizes="auto, (max-width: 582px) 100vw, 582px" /></p>
